| **[Taming Hallucinations: Boosting MLLMs' Video Understanding via Counterfactual Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24271v2)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made remarkable progress in video understanding. However, they suffer from a critical vulnerability: an over-reliance on language priors, which can lead to visual ungrounded hallucinations, especially when processing counterfactual videos that defy common sense. This limitation, stemming from the intrinsic data imbalance between text and video, is challenging to address due to the substantial cost of collecting and annotating counterfactual data. To address this, we introduce DualityForge, a novel counterfactual data synthesis framework that employs controllable, diffusion-based video editing to transform real-world videos into counterfactual scenarios. By embedding structured contextual information into the video editing and QA generation processes, the framework automatically produces high-quality QA pairs together with original-edited video pairs for contrastive training. Based on this, we build DualityVidQA, a large-scale video dataset designed to reduce MLLM hallucinations. In addition, to fully exploit the contrastive nature of our paired data, we propose Duality-Normalized Advantage Training (DNA-Train), a two-stage SFT-RL training regime where the RL phase applies pair-wise $\ell_1$ advantage normalization, thereby enabling a more stable and efficient policy optimization. Experiments on DualityVidQA-Test demonstrate that our method substantially reduces model hallucinations on counterfactual videos, yielding a relative improvement of 24.0% over the Qwen2.5-VL-7B baseline. Moreover, our approach achieves significant gains across both hallucination and general-purpose benchmarks, indicating strong generalization capability. We will open-source our dataset and code.</p></details> | 18 pages |
| **[More Powerful, Less Verifiable? Mitigating Hard-to-Verify Hallucinations in MLLM's Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32055v1)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) strengthens model capabilities through feedback from humans and environments. However, emerging evidence suggests that reward optimization can produce outputs whose errors are harder for humans to detect. To address this issue, we construct ITHV (the Image-Text Hallucination Verifiability Dataset), comprising 2,728 visually grounded instances that distinguish between obvious hallucinations, which humans readily detect, and elusive hallucinations, which humans struggle to identify. We then propose DAPO-V, an extension of DAPO that maximizes correctness subject to a budget on verifiability-aware risk. Specifically, DAPO-V combines a verifiability-aware reward with adaptive dual control, where larger penalties are assigned to incorrect responses on questions with lower human verification accuracy. An adaptive multiplier further adjusts the penalty strength according to risk-budget violations. Across three MLLM base models, DAPO-V improves overall accuracy while substantially improving accuracy on elusive hallucination questions by 5.78--9.72 percentage points over accuracy-only DAPO. We further test DAPO-V with a blinded human study, which shows that hallucinations are more readily detected in DAPO-V than those of the base model.</p></details> | 20 pages, 11 figures |
| **[Can MLLMs Reason About Visual Persuasion? Evaluating the Efficacy and Faithfulness of Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08965v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Persuasive visuals play a central role in advertising, public communication, and online media, making it increasingly important to understand whether an image is persuasive and why. However, current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have limited ability to reason about visual persuasion. Our analysis reveals that models often rely on a shortcut---selectively citing easily recognizable visual elements and treating their mere presence as evidence of persuasiveness---rather than reasoning over the diverse visual cues in an image to determine how and why they contribute to persuasiveness. To address this limitation, we propose (1) a fine-tuning approach that trains models on rationales reflecting diverse perspectives on visual persuasiveness, and (2) an evaluation framework that measures the faithfulness of models' rationales through three complementary metrics. Fine-tuning on multi-perspective rationales improves persuasiveness prediction and reasoning effectiveness. However, our evaluation framework reveals a discrepancy between prediction performance and rationale faithfulness, showing that higher prediction performance does not necessarily correspond to more faithful reasoning. These findings highlight the need to evaluate effectiveness and faithfulness separately and inform future directions for improving both the training and evaluation of visual persuasion reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Static Charts: Benchmarking Interactive Chart-to-Code Generation for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31345v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have achieved significant progress in chart-to-code generation. However, existing benchmarks mainly focus on static charts, overlooking interactive charts that enable richer and more expressive data exploration. Unlike static chart-to-code generation, which emphasizes visual consistency, interactive chart-to-code evaluation targets behavioral consistency, requiring chart states to evolve correctly under sequences of user interactions, thereby rendering traditional one-step evaluation paradigms ineffective. To address this gap, we introduce ChartInt, the first benchmark specifically designed for interactive chart-to-code generation. ChartInt consists of 2,779 carefully annotated examples covering common interaction patterns such as hover, highlight, and tooltips. Additionally, ChartInt incorporates editing tasks such as data updates, style adjustments, and cross-chart style transfer to expand real-world application scenarios. To enable reliable evaluation of interaction behavior, we propose a preset state snapshot protocol that predefines target interaction states and deterministically triggers interaction events, transforming dynamic behaviors into reproducible evaluation targets. Based on this protocol, we design a multi-dimensional evaluation framework measuring semantic and stylistic consistency. Experiments show that while current MLLMs perform well on static charts, their performance drops significantly on interactive tasks, highlighting the need for interaction-aware benchmarks.</p></details> | <details><summary>ACM W...</summary><p>ACM WSDM 2027 (Main Track), Our code and data are available at https://github.com/bianyh/ChartInt</p></details> |
| **[MuseBench: Benchmarking Intent-Level Audiovisual Arts Understanding in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30026v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Audiovisual arts encompass diverse creative disciplines, including cinema, visual arts, stage performance, and game design, where artistic meaning arises from deliberate combinations of visual, auditory, and narrative elements (e.g., fear amplified through claustrophobic framing, or grief conveyed through silence and lingering close-ups). Artistic understanding extends beyond recognizing what is depicted to reasoning about why it is expressed through particular creative choices. Despite progress in multimodal large language models (MLLMs), reasoning about artistic intent remains underexplored, as existing benchmarks largely measure perceptual recognition. To address this gap, we introduce MuseBench, a benchmark for evaluating intent-level artistic understanding in MLLMs. It comprises 4,016 questions derived from video essays spanning cinematic arts, static visual arts, stage performing arts, and game arts. To capture the open-ended nature of artistic analysis at scale, the benchmark combines single-select and variable-option multi-select questions. All questions are generated and refined through a four-phase iterative pipeline combining shortcut filtering, adversarial distractors, and expert validation. We evaluate 34 MLLM versions using either video and audio transcripts (V+T) or video, native audio, and text (V+A+T). The highest observed accuracy is 64.62% (V+A+T), trailing the 87.18% human expert baseline. Further analysis reveals that native-audio models score lowest on game arts, models often recover partial interpretations but struggle to identify complete correct option sets on multi-select items, and adaptive frame-selection models trail the strongest video-specific model. These results identify artistic knowledge and complete interpretation as challenges for audiovisual models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://musebench.github.io</p></details> |
| **[OmniV-Bench: Can MLLMs Comprehend Immersive Spatio-temporal Scenes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.31022v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Omnidirectional videos (ODVs) capture dynamic environments with a full $360^\circ$ field of view, providing rich spatial context and temporal dynamics for applications such as virtual reality (VR), augmented reality (AR), and embodied intelligence. Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made great progress in spatial and temporal understanding, yet their ability to comprehend the immersive 360 spatio-temporal information conveyed by ODVs remains largely unexplored. To bridge this gap, we first introduce OmniV-Bench, a Benchmark designed to comprehensively evaluate the capabilities of MLLMs on spatio-temporal Omnidirectional Video understanding. OmniV-Bench contains 4,123 high-quality ODVs collected from diverse scenes, along with 8,923 human-annotated question-answer pairs spanning 11 fine-grained tasks across three categories, i.e., general, spatial, and spatio-temporal understanding. Extensive benchmark experiments reveal that the spatio-temporal complexity of ODVs poses significant challenges for current MLLMs. To address these limitations, we further propose OmniV-Harness, an agentic harness that enhances ODV understanding through viewpoint-guided evidence initialization and task-relevant evidence acquisition with adaptive tool use. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that OmniV-Harness consistently improves immersive spatio-temporal reasoning ability of MLLMs. Both OmniV-Bench and OmniV-Harness will be released upon publication.</p></details> |  |
| **[TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive segmentation models turn a few clicks into a segmentation prediction across domains, so annotators only decide where the prediction still needs correction. Annotation thus becomes a loop of inspecting the prediction and clicking again. Each step of this loop is a visual judgment, which suits general-purpose multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents. In this work, we focus on letting these agents run this loop on 3D volumes. However, the MLLM in such an agent takes only 2D images as input, so the agent must observe the 3D volume in 2D. Since the loop calls the MLLM many times, this observation must be token-compact enough to keep every call cheap, yet informative enough to show error regions in the 3D prediction. We meet both needs with tri-planes, the three orthogonal planes that cross at one point in the volume. We show, both theoretically and empirically, that tri-planes are context-efficient yet keep sufficient detail to show error regions in the 3D prediction. Building on tri-planes, we introduce TriSegAgent, an MLLM agent that refines the 3D prediction iteratively. Within the agent, the MLLM only judges the prediction on tri-planes, while a visual harness turns each judgment into a 3D action in code. To make the loop reliable, the harness places each click at an exact voxel in the volume and checks whether to accept the 3D prediction. To evaluate the agent, we build a diverse benchmark of 690 regions of interest (ROIs) from 35 public medical datasets across three imaging modalities. With frontier MLLMs, TriSegAgent reaches an overall Dice about 10 points above the strongest baseline, and its lead is even larger on targets below 3 cm. With flash-level MLLMs, it outperforms a recent 3D segmentation agent built on a frontier MLLM.</p></details> |  |
| **[GraphSeer: Multimodal Knowledge Graph Relational Image Retrieval with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29470v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal knowledge graphs (MMKGs) store entities with photos and names and link them by facts. MMKG methods predict links between known entity IDs, and image retrievers, from CLIP to MLLM embedders, return entities that look like a query photo or match its description. Neither answers a common question about a new photo: which graph entity is related to the one shown, such as the team of a pictured player or the city of a landmark. We call this knowledge graph relational image retrieval (KRIR): given a photo and a natural-language relation, retrieve the related MMKG entity, which rarely resembles the photo, whether the depicted entity is in the graph (known) or not (unseen). We build three benchmarks from YAGO, Wikidata and in-the-wild OVEN photos. The task hides two hops: recognising which graph entity is shown, and recalling what it is connected to. GraphSeer bridges them inside one multimodal LLM: it links the photo to the graph, reads the facts of the best match, and retrieves with the hidden state at which the model is about to say the answer. An unseen entity has no facts of its own, but every candidate answer does, so a listwise verifier reads the other end of the graph: it re-ranks the top candidates by their facts and photos in one forward pass, and the facts matter far more than the photos. With Qwen3.5-27B, GraphSeer improves MRR on known entities by 24.2-70.0% over an MLLM embedder fine-tuned on the same data with the same budget. When it also fuses and verifies the embedder's candidates, it improves MRR on unseen entities by 12.5-25.6%, and on known entities it beats KG-completion models given the ground-truth entity ID.</p></details> |  |
| **[Remote-Sensing City Layout Extraction with MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16484v2)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Remote-sensing systems usually describe urban content with detection boxes, semantic masks, or vector boundaries. Such outputs locate classes and support image-plane scoring, yet they do not by themselves constitute an executable layout that retains object identities, typed relations, topology, and regeneration rules. Code-as-City instead casts urban-layout extraction from a single top-down image as constrained code generation with a multimodal large language model (MLLM). An image model first produces an aligned five-class semantic layout prior. Three ordered MLLM passes use the image and this prior to recover roads, land-cover regions and relations, and buildings. Deterministic normalization converts the accumulated records into a city graph and a restricted layout program. Executing the program creates a renderable 3D city layout and an orthographic semantic projection over shared geometry. The projection admits pixel-level comparison with remote-sensing masks, while named objects, relations, and editing operations remain available for synchronized regeneration of both views. Evaluated on the 100 scenes of CityLayout-100, the complete framework obtains 41.1% mean intersection-over-union and 48.3% global intersection-over-union. This result provides quantitative evidence that visual observations can be translated into inspectable, editable city code with coupled planar and 3D outputs.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Accepted to IEEE APGARSS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Attention Maps: Understanding and Improving Medical MLLMs via Visual Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29361v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current MLLM methods largely focus on whether models attend to the right visual regions, implicitly treating spatial attention map as the primary key of effective visual perception. To examine this assumption, we introduce a medical VQA dataset with paired non-contrast (NC) and contrast-enhanced (CE) images that share corresponding anatomy while providing different cues. We show that this view is incomplete: across paired NC and CE medical images, MLLMs often exhibit similar attention patterns despite producing contrasting answers, while controlled bidirectional interventions induce substantially larger prediction changes on visual values than attention weights. These results identify the visual content retrieved through values as a critical factor in improving perception beyond spatial attention alone. Guided by this finding, we propose VIVID (VIsual Value Intervention Distillation), which uses enhanced visual values to identify and refine teacher corrections aligned with useful visual evidence to improve on-policy distillation from CE to NC images. Across six internal and external test sets, VIVID consistently outperforms existing on-policy distillation methods across different model scales and architectures, requires only NC images at inference, and maintains strong zero-shot performance on general-domain VQA benchmarks. The project page is available at https://qiuzyc.github.io/VIVID.</p></details> |  |
| **[Counting Symbols on Technical Drawings: A Benchmark and Verified Rewards for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29309v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Today's MLLMs can count objects in natural images. However, when asking them how many bolt holes appear in a mechanical drawing or how many nitrogen atoms are in a chemical structure, they often struggle. Counting in technical drawings remains challenging, as objects can be small, densely packed, or even implied by drawing conventions. To study this problem, we introduce CountDrawBench, a benchmark including floor plans, electrical schematics, mechanical drawings, and molecular structures, with 1,574 counting questions and instance-level annotations where available. Besides evaluation, we introduce Code-Verified RL, a reinforcement learning framework that teaches MLLMs to count with executable feedback. Our method combines three complementary rewards: a count reward that checks the final answer, an instance-matching reward that verifies object locations, and a zoom-based reward that checks counting consistency across local image regions. All three are integrated into a unified GRPO objective. Extensive experiments reveal that even leading MLLMs struggle on CountDrawBench, with the best evaluated model achieving only 59.3% accuracy. Our method improves Qwen3.5-9B from 49.3% to 54.3% accuracy, outperforming all evaluated open-source baselines. These results establish CountDrawBench as a challenging testbed for technical drawing understanding and demonstrate the value of code-verified rewards in these tasks. Code and data will be released upon acceptance.</p></details> | 15 pages, 8 figures |
| **[MM-DataLoop: Closing the Data-Side Loop for Recursive Self-Improvement in MLLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29254v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) offers a path toward improving models through repeated evaluation and revision. We study its data side in multimodal language model (MLLM) post-training, where heterogeneous operations and cross-round evidence must be coordinated. MMDataLoop organizes data interventions into five operation families and connects them through an Observe-Diagnose-Plan-Execute-Verify-Reflect cycle with shared rule memory. Across 34 candidate rounds on Qwen3.5-0.8B and five benchmarks, we hold the base initialization, shared training schedule and evaluation protocol fixed while varying data recipes. The five-benchmark mean reaches 63.04 at R33, up 5.32 points from the zero-shot baseline; R29, R32, and R33 form a close top cluster within 0.09 points. In eightcandidate comparisons, guided search reaches the preselected target in six candidates and identifies the top observed candidate, while random arms show higher means and lower dispersion. The selected R29 recipe also scores above two full-pool baselines under the same epoch schedule. Together, these findings establish MM-DataLoop as a traceable workflow and provide an empirical basis for studying how data-side decisions shape outcomes across MLLM post-training recipes.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Single Expert: Harmonizing Diverse Visual Priors in MLLMs for Spatial Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15054v2)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated substantial promise in spatial understanding. Existing works typically incorporate prior knowledge extracted from a pre-trained foundation model to further enhance the spatial awareness of MLLMs. In this paper, we first reveal that when integrating diverse foundation models into MLLMs, different models provide complementary spatial priors that benefit different tasks. Motivated by this, we propose $\textbf{ViPS}$, a novel multi-model prior framework designed to fully unleash the potential of incorporating multiple $\textbf{Vi}$sual $\textbf{P}$riors from diverse models into MLLMs for $\textbf{S}$patial understanding. Specifically, ViPS introduces an Efficient Prior Proxy to generate multiple foundational priors with minimal inference overhead, and a Dynamic Prior Fusion mechanism to achieve harmonious and context-aware prior fusion and injection from the prior proxies. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ViPS successfully harmonizes diverse visual priors, establishing new state-of-the-art performance across multiple complex spatial reasoning and 3D spatial understanding benchmarks. Project page: https://visual-ai.github.io/vips</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Moment-Video: Diagnosing Temporal Fidelity of Video MLLMs on Momentary Visual Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02522v3)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress in general and long-form video understanding, yet their ability to capture and use brief, answer-critical visual evidence remains underexplored. Momentary actions or state transitions may determine an answer while occupying only a small fraction of the video. Performance on broad video understanding tasks alone does not establish whether models can reliably identify and use this temporally localized evidence. We introduce Moment-Video, a benchmark for evaluating temporal fidelity through momentary visual event understanding. Questions are constructed around visually observable events or event sequences, requiring models to notice, count, describe, or reason about transient evidence. The benchmark contains 1,000 human-verified video-QA pairs across seven domains and 25 subcategories, covering four tasks: Temporal Occurrence, Temporal Counting, Action Description, and Temporal Reasoning. We evaluate 34 proprietary and open-source MLLMs. Under the main evaluation settings, the best-performing model, Seed-2.0-Pro, achieves 39.6% overall accuracy, while most open-source models remain below 25%. Diagnostic analyses show that denser frame sampling improves some models, but substantial errors persist in an audited subset even when human review judges the sampled visual evidence sufficient to answer. Performance also declines on longer videos, suggesting additional temporal-localization challenges. By exposing these limitations, Moment-Video provides a diagnostic benchmark to guide the evaluation and development of video MLLMs that more reliably understand brief, answer-critical visual events.</p></details> | <details><summary>36 pa...</summary><p>36 pages, 12 figures, 20 tables</p></details> |
| **[EVIDENT: Routing MLLM Adaptation through Entity-Grounded Visual Evidence for Cross-Domain Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26104v3)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fine-tuning MLLMs for Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) often improves in-domain performance but degrades sharply under domain shift. In this work, we find that this failure is primarily driven not just by unseen query concepts, but by visual domain shift, which prevents the model from coupling its learned temporal localization knowledge with its inherent entity-attention capability. To address this, we introduce EVIDENT, a parameter-efficient adaptation framework that anchors temporal grounding in the inherent entity-attention of pre-trained MLLMs by routing VTG adaptation through explicit visual entity evidence. EVIDENT consists of three components: (i) an Entity Bottleneck Adapter that transforms dense visual tokens into compact entity-level slots, (ii) an Entity-Binding Distillation loss that instills objectness priors into the semantically unstructured MLLM visual space, guiding each slot to bind to a coherent entity, and (iii) an Entity-to-eVidence gating mechanism that leverages the captured entities as evidence, steering the model to localize moments containing query-relevant entities. Together, these components enable VTG fine-tuning to rely on entity-grounded evidence rather than brittle dataset shortcuts. Experiments on cross-domain VTG benchmarks show that EVIDENT consistently improves out-of-domain robustness while preserving competitive in-domain performance with modest parameter overhead. These results suggest that entity-level grounding is an effective inductive bias for generalizable temporal localization.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://mreraser.github.io/EVIDENT_project_page/</p></details> |
| **[SPACE: Source-free Proxy Anchor Concept Erasure for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09868v2)** | 2026-11-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face growing privacy risks and regulatory constraints, machine unlearning (MU) has emerged as a crucial solution for removing sensitive data while preserving model performance. However, existing MU methods typically rely on visual data of the target concepts, which is often unavailable due to strict data retention policies, thus creating a demand for source-free unlearning approaches that operate without access to the target data. In this work, we propose Source-free Proxy Anchor Concept Erasure (SPACE), the first source-free unlearning framework specialized for MLLMs. SPACE consists of two stages: (1) Text-Guided Proxy Anchor Selection (TPAS), which retrieves semantically aligned proxy anchors from the shared feature space. (2) Dual-Constraint Semantic Isolation (DCSI), which optimizes these anchors to indirectly erase target concepts. DCSI confines updates to the null space of retained knowledge, ensuring structural integrity. We theoretically prove that SPACE strictly bounds the perturbation on retained knowledge and maximizes feature spectral entropy, thereby maintaining the model's performance. Furthermore, extensive experiments across six datasets show that SPACE achieves performance comparable to that of state-of-the-art data-dependent methods, validating its effectiveness in source-free MU scenarios. The source code will be released.</p></details> |  |
| **[WM^MLLM: Turning MLLMs into World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24193v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video world models keep the world in the context of a generator, so a revisit is synthesized again and earlier edits can drift or disappear. We present WM^MLLM, the first interactive world model built on multimodal large language models (MLLMs), for persistent, editable world generation from a single image. WM^MLLM keeps the world state outside every generator, as a panorama with a revision history, and lets the MLLMs write it: they turn each request into operations on this state, select where a local edit is written, and check every candidate before it becomes the next state. Image generators only propose views, and video only presents them. Users can walk to new places and return, edit objects through text or a box, place subjects, and change lighting, weather or season. By construction, a revisit at the same revision reproduces the earlier view exactly, a local edit leaves every panorama pixel outside its region unchanged, and each request starts from the result of the previous one. Against six world model baselines on MBench-A, WM^MLLM has the highest mean on all four environment-recovery metrics (Epipolar 91.5 versus 0.1 to 74.0) and completes the most camera round trips (85.3% versus at most 50.9%). It has the best value in all 15 CompBench columns against 15 baselines. On 112 local edits judged blind, selecting the region to write after generation raises completion from 34.8% to 83.0%. On 258 WBench and MBench-A items, all 46 revisits recover their earlier views, and all 97 local edits that pass the check leave panorama pixels outside their region unchanged.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 14 figures, 8 tables; project page: https://ztmotalee.github.io/wmmllm/</p></details> |
| **[Multimodal Information Fusion for Chart Understanding: A Survey of MLLMs -- Evolution, Limitations, and Cognitive Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10138v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chart understanding is a quintessential information fusion task, requiring the seamless integration of graphical and textual data to extract meaning. The advent of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has revolutionized this domain, yet the landscape of MLLM-based chart analysis remains fragmented and lacks systematic organization. This survey provides a comprehensive roadmap of this nascent frontier by structuring the domain's core components. We begin by analyzing the fundamental challenges of fusing visual and linguistic information in charts. We then categorize downstream tasks and datasets, introducing a novel taxonomy of canonical and non-canonical benchmarks to highlight the field's expanding scope. Subsequently, we present a comprehensive evolution of methodologies, tracing the progression from classic deep learning techniques to state-of-the-art MLLM paradigms that leverage sophisticated fusion strategies. By critically examining the limitations of current models, particularly their perceptual and reasoning deficits, we identify promising future directions, including advanced alignment techniques and reinforcement learning for cognitive enhancement. This survey aims to equip researchers and practitioners with a structured understanding of how MLLMs are transforming chart information fusion and to catalyze progress toward more robust and reliable systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scenes as an Attack Surface: Uncovering Scene-Grounded Safety Failures in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14094v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing jailbreak attacks have exposed serious safety weaknesses in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Most prior attacks rely on specially constructed visual inputs to carry or support harmful intent. In this paper, we uncover a different vulnerability: task-relevant scene semantics alone can shift an MLLM from refusal to compliance, even when the visual input introduces no additional attack instructions and the original task intent remains unchanged. We formalize this failure as a scene-grounded visual safety vulnerability and introduce SceneSafetyBench to investigate it across eight risk categories and nine white- and black-box MLLMs. Our analysis reveals that this failure is associated with how task-relevant visual evidence influences downstream safety representations, suggesting that visual grounding alone cannot fully explain the observed safety shift. Building on this finding, we propose Scene-Grounded Attacks (SGA), which preserve the original task while constructing validated scene-grounded queries and combining their independent responses. Across nine target MLLMs, SGA achieves an average attack success rate (ASR) of 86.17%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 8.55 percentage points and achieving the highest ASR on eight models. Our results identify scene semantics as an overlooked attack surface in MLLMs and highlight a growing tension between stronger visual grounding and robust multimodal safety alignment.</p></details> | <details><summary>The a...</summary><p>The authors have identified limitations in the mechanistic analysis presented in this version. In particular, the current evidence is insufficient to fully substantiate the proposed explanation of scene-grounded safety failures. We are withdrawing this version to prevent potentially overstated conclusions</p></details> |
| **[SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23483v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.</p></details> | Technical report |
| **[VIVID: Beyond Static Multi-Image Reasoning with Localized Visual Differences and Textual Entity Dynamics in Video MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23153v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video question answering under tight frame budgets requires preserving localized motion and event order, yet current video multimodal large language models (MLLMs) reduce comprehension to static multi-image reasoning by discarding unselected frames and encoding keyframes independently: (1) Spatially, whole-frame pooling dilutes small moving targets into static backgrounds; (2) Temporally, dropping intermediate frames or averaging tokens erases inter-frame motion, confusing direction, speed, and order. We propose VIVID (Visual Inter-frame Variations and Individual Dynamics), which moves beyond static snapshots like human vision: keyframes capture core states while inter-frame Patch Diffs preserve neighboring changes within $K_f$ keyframes. Autonomous Delta-Driven Keyframe Planning (ADKP) selects anchors from a $3\text{ fps}$ pool and feeds surrounding patch differences into Dual-Road Change Memory (DRCM), forming Dynamic Tokens at zero extra anchor tokens alongside a camera-stabilized Active Motion Region of Interest (ROI) and chronological Entity State Chains. Adding $0.22\%$ trainable parameters to a frozen $27\text{B}$ backbone, VIVID-Small ($50$ examples, $\sim 1.5$ GPU hours) beats all eleven baselines in overall accuracy across $8,083$ questions and trails only VIVID-Large ($3,600$ examples), which leads on MotionBench, Video-MME, and TempCompass.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 2 figures, 5 tables. Code: https://github.com/step-out/VIVID</p></details> |
| **[Visual Contextual Attack: Jailbreaking MLLMs with Image-Driven Context Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02844v3)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the emergence of strong vision language capabilities, multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated tremendous potential for real-world applications. However, the security vulnerabilities exhibited by the visual modality pose significant challenges to deploying such models in open-world environments. Recent studies have successfully induced harmful responses from target MLLMs by encoding harmful textual semantics directly into visual inputs. However, in these approaches, the visual modality primarily serves as a trigger for unsafe behavior, often exhibiting semantic ambiguity and lacking grounding in realistic scenarios. In this work, we define a novel setting: vision-centric jailbreak, where visual information serves as a necessary component in constructing a complete and realistic jailbreak context. Building on this setting, we propose the VisCo (Visual Contextual) Attack. VisCo fabricates contextual dialogue using four distinct vision-focused strategies, dynamically generating auxiliary images when necessary to construct a vision-centric jailbreak scenario. To maximize attack effectiveness, it incorporates automatic toxicity obfuscation and semantic refinement to produce a final attack prompt that reliably triggers harmful responses from the target black-box MLLMs. Specifically, VisCo achieves a toxicity score of 4.78 and an Attack Success Rate (ASR) of 85% on MM-SafetyBench against GPT-4o, significantly outperforming the baseline, which achieves a toxicity score of 2.48 and an ASR of 22.2%. Code: https://github.com/Dtc7w3PQ/Visco-Attack.</p></details> |  |
| **[Diagnosing and Correcting Bias in MLLM for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36210v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-video question answering is challenging since the answer often hinges on a few decisive moments scattered throughout the video, while memory constraints force Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to sample frames at extremely low rates, resulting in sparsity that risks omitting key evidence. Though aggressively enlarging the context size is intuitive, we argue that performance remains fundamentally constrained by the bias, i.e., the misleading cues caused by spurious linguistic correlations and salient yet irrelevant visual observations. In this paper, we first introduce S-MME, a benchmark designed to systematically diagnose shortcut-biased behavior in MLLMs. To mitigate this failure, we further propose a training-free framework, Counterfactual Long-video Evidence-Aware Reasoning (CLEAR). By treating the full video-question pair as a complete view, CLEAR estimates bias effects via augmented views by only keeping the question or locally salient visual clips. Then, we intervene hidden states to mitigate the discrepancy at inference such that the correct answer can be predicted confidently. With experimental studies across five benchmarks, we show that our method is generalizable and can be integrated in different MLLMs, yielding consistent gains in prediction and robustness. We hope CLEAR can offer a complementary direction in test-time scaling for long-video understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026, with acknowledgent details updated. Code:https://github.com/Xiaoqiovo/CLEAR</p></details> |
| **[OmniTongue: A Comprehensive MLLM Benchmark for Automated Tongue Diagnosis in Traditional Chinese Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22652v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have excelled in Western medical imaging, yet their potential in Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) tongue diagnosis remains systematically unevaluated. Tongue diagnosis requires sophisticated global and local perception, serving as a rigorous touchstone for assessing the visual understanding depth of MLLMs. However, existing datasets are limited by single dimensions, coarse granularity, and ambiguous labels, hindering the evaluation of authentic clinical capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce OmniTongue, the first comprehensive, fine-grained Visual Question Answering benchmark for TCM tongue diagnosis. This benchmark integrates 1,351 expert-validated images and 21,616 instruction pairs. Its core design features 16 clinical dimensions for multidimensional evaluation, region-specific annotations for pathological localization, and 66 fine-grained labels for precise diagnostic representation. Extensive evaluations of existing MLLMs reveal significant performance gaps, demonstrating that medical-specific models lack domain advantages, while universally poor Macro-F1 scores expose a reliance on statistical shortcuts rather than genuine understanding of tongue pathology. These findings not only highlight the current limitations of MLLMs in interpreting regional medical features but also underscore OmniTongue's significance for developing robust automated tongue diagnosis models. The code and dataset will be made publicly available in a future release.</p></details> | <details><summary>11 pa...</summary><p>11 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[From Perception to Simulation: Unveiling and Empowering Mental Spatial Dynamics in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22541v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Spatial reasoning is a fundamental component of world understanding for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While existing research has advanced the perception of spatial relations in static scenes, the domain of dynamic spatial simulation remains relatively unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce MentalGrid, a benchmark focused on spatial reasoning tasks that require models to simulate evolving spatial states without intermediate visual feedback. Extensive evaluation on our benchmark reveals a significant deficiency in the spatial simulation capabilities of existing models. Specifically, we find that performance is constrained by perceptual limitations in simple simulation scenarios and hindered by inadequate multi-step reasoning as complexity increases. To address these challenges, we propose a three-stage curriculum learning framework utilizing 16k specialized spatial reasoning samples. This approach yields SpatialWalker, which achieves a 33.8% average improvement over its base model, surpassing proprietary models like Gemini-3-Flash in spatial simulation. Furthermore, SpatialWalker demonstrates robust generalization, maintaining a 4.9% average gain on out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrating the pivotal role of spatial simulation as a fundamental building block for broader spatial intelligence.</p></details> | <details><summary>Findi...</summary><p>Findings of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[Dressed in Authority: Practical Black-Box Jailbreaking of MLLMs via Visual Trust Bias](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22292v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are rapidly advancing, yet their safety remains a critical concern, especially under jailbreak attempts. Existing MLLM jailbreaks either inject fragile adversarial perturbations into irrelevant images or synthesize toxic visual cues to achieve text-driven attack intents. In contrast, a more practical MLLM jailbreak emphasizes enhancing attack efficacy directly on naturally occurring harmful image-text pairs. We address this problem by proposing Visual Trust Bias Attack (VTBA), a new jailbreak framework that exploits a previously overlooked attack surface: MLLMs exhibit an intrinsic trust bias toward authoritative visual features, which enables transferable and stealthy jailbreaks. VTBA constructs a bank of authoritative templates, matches each input pair to the most relevant one, and integrates the harmful intent into the selected layout, thereby leveraging credible visual cues to override safety refusals while preserving naturalness. We further construct a dataset of 2,500 naturally occurring image-text pairs to evaluate jailbreaks in this practical multimodal setting. Extensive experiments show that our method consistently achieves high attack success rates across strong MLLMs, revealing a severe and previously underexplored weakness in current multimodal safety alignment. Warning: This paper may contain sensitive content.</p></details> | 10 pages, 9 figures |
| **[SARVIA: Fast-Adapting the Strong SAR Foundation Models That Are Already in Your MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22273v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) foundation models provide transferable representations for diverse perception tasks in all-weather Earth observation. However, their development often relies on costly large-scale SAR pretraining, raising the question of whether such extensive domain-specific pretraining is necessary. We find that modern multimodal large language models (MLLMs) already provide visual encoders that can outperform dedicated SAR foundation models as downstream initializations. This motivates a new paradigm: reuse general visual capabilities and devote domain training to brief specialization. We therefore introduce SARVIA, an adaptive post-pretraining method that specializes modern MLLMs using self-supervised visual questions with computable answers. These questions are constructed from unlabeled observations through general image tasks that capture spatial relations and SAR-specific tasks that exploit native measurements and SAR--optical correspondence. A shared answer-prediction objective jointly trains low-rank adapters across visual and language components, with loss-driven task sampling. We then export the adapted visual encoder as a standalone backbone. With 600 adaptation steps and 2 GPU-hours, SARVIA outperforms the compared foundation models on all six SAR detection and segmentation benchmarks. Compared with dedicated SAR pretraining, SARVIA reduces domain-training costs by orders of magnitude while improving visual transfer across architectures and scales. This new paradigm shifts the emphasis from repeated, costly pretraining to a reusable specialization process that can benefit from advances in open MLLMs. Dataset and code: https://github.com/zcablii/SARVIA.</p></details> |  |
| **[TIVS: Mapping the Sensitivity Landscape of Visual Token Transformations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19745v2)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent efforts to improve the efficiency of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have explored visual token pruning, quantization, and feature compression. However, the properties that determine whether transformed visual tokens remain usable by a frozen decoder are not well characterized. We introduce \textbf{TIVS (Token Identity Versus Statistics)}, a training-free intervention framework that separately studies sequence permutation, token retention, numerical precision, and statistical replacement under controlled conditions. On 192 POPE questions, three token permutations change TinyLLaVA accuracy by only $-0.17$ percentage points on average, while changing 4.69\% of individual predictions. Diagonal and rank-64 Gaussian replacements decrease accuracy by 25.0 and 28.1 points on the same subset. Further experiments with exact moment constraints, quantization error, token retention, and additional visual tasks reveal how strongly the effects depend on the original feature values and the evaluation setting. These findings provide an empirical basis for assessing visual token transformations in frozen MLLMs and designing more reliable multimodal interfaces.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 12 figures, 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Token Stacking: Multi-scale Visual Modulation and Adaptive Fused-feature Calibration for MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21168v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have advanced rapidly and follow two main paradigms: one involves concatenating visual and textual tokens for input into Large Language Model (LLM), while the other injects visual knowledge into Feed-Forward Networks (FFNs) of the LLM. However, the former introduces a large number of redundant visual tokens, substantially increasing computational overhead. The latter reduces sequence length but removes images from the input stage entirely, which may hinder the activation of image-related knowledge. Moreover, naively injecting visual knowledge into the FFNs could induce activation distribution shifts due to cross-modal semantic discrepancies. To address these challenges, we develop Beyond Token Stacking (BTS), a novel visual enhancement framework for MLLMs. Specifically, we propose a Multi-scale Visual Modulation (MVM) mechanism that injects multi-scale visual information at the input stage via modulation without increasing the token sequence length. Furthermore, we develop an Adaptive Fused-feature Calibration (AFC) module that performs per-channel affine calibration to decouple visual injection from distribution alignment in the FFN layers, improving multimodal fusion stability while preserving language priors. Experiments show that our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance.</p></details> |  |
| **[MemDoctor: Stage-wise Error Attribution and Repair for MLLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.21019v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) now assist users across sessions of images and text, carried by an external memory of what was said and shown. When such an assistant answers wrongly, the failure usually began earlier, in how a record was written, updated or retrieved, and the answer alone does not say where. We formalize multimodal memory as four stages, Write, Update, Retrieve and Answer, and define nine error types across them. A stage-resolved analysis on Mem-Gallery traces most failures to a source stage, and most of those lie before the answer. Injecting known errors against fact-preserving control edits, we then follow each tainted record downstream. Early errors survive to the answer as apparent evidence, and hurt most when no other record holds the fact. A diagnosis that trusts the quoted evidence therefore blames the stage where the error surfaces, not the one where it began. We propose MemDoctor, in which the MLLM that built the memory diagnoses its own failures. It traces each needed fact backward through the four stages, accepts a diagnosis only when the cited evidence is found in memory, and repairs that stage before answering again. On a 4B backbone, MemDoctor improves accuracy on both Mem-Gallery and MemLens, outperforming all evaluated 8B baselines, and the same loop also improves MuRAG and A-Mem.</p></details> |  |
| **[ProSE: Probing Reconstruction Error for Semantic-guided Expert Skipping in MoE-MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.20885v1)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mixture-of-Experts Multimodal Large Language Models (MoE-MLLMs) offer scalable inference through sparse expert activation, yet substantial computational redundancy remains among the activated experts. Existing expert skipping methods typically calibrate thresholds based on target skipping ratios or final-output divergence, overlooking the accumulation of skipping-induced error across layers and the varying semantic importance of individual tokens. To address that, we propose ProSE, a training-free, semantic-guided expert skipping framework that probes layer-wise reconstruction error to promote representation consistency compared with default activation. Specifically, we construct query-aware semantic masks to identify salient visual tokens and progressively calibrate modality-specific skipping thresholds for each MoE layer. By minimizing masked reconstruction error under a given budget, ProSE maintains semantic consistency after skipping and allows adaptive expert allocation under limited activation budgets. Extensive experiments on 9 benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements on average over existing expert skipping methods, particularly under aggressive skipping settings. ProSE maintains over 96\% performance on KimiVL-A3B-Inst with 83\% expert skipping and accelerates prefilling by $1.7\times$.</p></details> | 8 pages |
| **[MI-Pruner: Crossmodal Mutual Information-guided Token Pruner for Efficient MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03072v2)** | 2026-11-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The redundancy of visual tokens in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) motivates training-free token pruning techniques for efficient inference. In this paper, we propose MI-Pruner, which derives Pointwise Mutual Information (PMI) based relevance scores in the projection space to reflect crossmodal relevance and intra-modal redundancy. Grounded in information theory, our approach identifies the most informative tokens by explicitly measuring feature-level dependency. Without reliance on internal attention maps or architectural modifications, MI-Pruner can be seamlessly applied to any pretrained MLLMs for inference acceleration. Extensive experiments on LLaVA1.5 and Qwen-series demonstrate that our approach achieves a favorable performance-efficiency trade-off. Specifically, we preserve ${>}97\%$ performance on LLaVA1.5-7B at a pruning rate of 88.9\%.</p></details> | 9 pages |
| **[GRASP: Guided Region-Aware Sparse Prompting for Adapting MLLMs to Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17089v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in visual question answering tasks. However, directly applying existing fine-tuning methods to remote sensing (RS) images often leads to issues such as overfitting on background noise or neglecting target details. This is primarily due to the large-scale variations, sparse target distributions, and complex regional semantic features inherent in RS images. These challenges limit the effectiveness of MLLMs in RS tasks. To address these challenges, we propose a parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) strategy called Guided Region-Aware Sparse Prompting (GRASP). GRASP introduces spatially structured soft prompts associated with spatial blocks extracted from a frozen visual token grid. Through a question-guided sparse fusion mechanism, GRASP dynamically aggregates task-specific context into a compact global prompt, enabling the model to focus on relevant regions while filtering out background noise. Extensive experiments on multiple RSVQA benchmarks show that GRASP achieves competitive performance compared to existing fine-tuning and prompt-based methods while maintaining high parameter efficiency.</p></details> |  |
| **[XLSAR-Bench: Towards Extremely Large SAR Imagery Understanding of MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19364v1)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid development of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) has highlighted the need for comprehensive benchmarks to assess their capabilities and limitations in remote sensing (RS). However, evaluating MLLMs in large-size synthetic aperture radar (SAR) scenarios remains challenging, as understanding complete scenes requires both global semantic interpretation and fine-grained target perception. Existing SAR benchmarks predominantly rely on small image patches, limiting their ability to assess these capabilities jointly. To address these limitations, we present XLSAR-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating the perception and reasoning capabilities of MLLMs in large-scene SAR imagery. XLSAR-Bench comprises 405 images with an average size of 7153$\times$7153 pixels, ten tasks spanning visual question answering, visual grounding, and detailed image captioning, with all evaluation samples meticulously annotated manually, assisted by a semi-automatic captioner. | **[Generate, Align, and Restore: MLLM-Empowered Diffusion for Generalizable Low-light Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.19285v1)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-light image enhancement (LLIE) is essential to numerous vision applications operating under adverse illumination. Despite substantial advances, real-world nighttime enhancement remains challenging due to the scarcity of strictly aligned day-night pairs and complex degradations involving extreme darkness and heterogeneous artificial light sources, leading to fragile generalization. To overcome these challenges, we propose MrLIE (MLLM-empowered reference Low-light Image Enhancement), a novel reference-based unsupervised LLIE framework that synergizes the generative and reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) with conditional diffusion. MrLIE employs the MLLM as a high-fidelity reference synthesizer and diagnostic critic, iteratively generating, diagnosing, and correcting normal-light references directly from degraded nighttime observations. Based on this, our method governs the enhancement process through two complementary mechanisms, in which a confidence offset module rectifies spatial-geometric inconsistencies for reference reliability and a structure-anchored injection scheme harmonizes aligned reference cues, low-light structural constraints, and ControlNet additive anchoring during the denoising process. | **[Can MLLMs Reason over Surround-View Driving Videos? A Multi-View Autonomous Driving QA Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15225v2)** | 2026-11-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Surround-view video understanding in autonomous driving is a crucial capability for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to achieve reliable driving reasoning. We design 12 categories of QA tasks that cover the complete reasoning pipeline from low-level visual perception to high-level driving decision-making, enabling a systematic evaluation of MLLMs surround-view spatio-temporal understanding and reasoning capabilities in complex driving environments. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of 23 open-source, closed-source, and expert MLLMs on MVDriveBench. The results show that current MLLMs still exhibit insufficient surround-view spatio-temporal reasoning ability. | **[Zoom Where the Answer Looks: Cross-Resolution Self-Distillation for MLLMs under Visual-Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32664v2)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Under the small per-view visual-token budgets of efficient deployment, the fine text of documents, charts and infographics becomes illegible to multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Zooming in recovers it, but the region must be chosen before the model answers. Existing methods decode an answer or a box first, at the cost of generation, or read the attention available before answering, which localizes the evidence only in top layers whose depth varies with model size. Learned region proposers instead distill the attention of the model's answers, but on the same budgeted view. Yet this attention is precise only on a view the model can read: on Qwen3-VL-4B, a window around its peak covers 83% of the evidence on a high-resolution view but 54% on the budgeted view. Since answers and high-resolution views are available during training, we introduce LookZoom, a zooming method that trains a dedicated look token by cross-resolution self-distillation of the answer's attention: with the MLLM frozen, the token learns to attend on the budgeted view, before any answer, where the model's own correct answers attend on a high-resolution view. The region around its attention peak is the window to zoom into, and reinforcement learning then trains the MLLM to answer from both views. With 98 visual tokens per view on five text-rich benchmarks, LookZoom surpasses attention-guided zooming by 20-24 points and grounding-based zooming and learned crop routing by 12-15 points on Qwen3-VL at three scales, and it outperforms learned region proposers at every budget from 98 to 392 tokens. On Qwen3-VL, the look token alone, with the MLLM frozen, already surpasses every baseline that leaves the language model unchanged, and the recipe carries over to InternVL3.5 and Qwen2.5-VL.</p></details> |  |
| **[BusterX++: Towards Unified Cross-Modal AI-Generated Content Detection and Explanation with MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.14632v5)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid advancement of generative AI has substantially improved image and video synthesis, amplifying the risk of multimodal visual misinformation. Recent MLLMs have shown promise for transparent AI-generated content detection through reasoning and explanation, yet existing approaches largely treat image and video forensics as isolated tasks, leaving cross-modal synergies underexplored. To address this, we present \textbf{BusterX++}, a unified MLLM for joint image and video detection with interpretable reasoning. We also introduce \textbf{GenBuster-Bench++}, a curated benchmark containing balanced image and video samples spanning recent generation models and diverse real-world scenarios, with synthetic images and videos screened using the same human difficulty criterion. Using this controlled setting, we revisit the widely adopted SFT+RL post-training paradigm. Notably, in the unified image-video setting, our results demonstrate that a single-stage pure RL strategy driven primarily by sparse outcome rewards outperforms a strong SFT+RL baseline. We observe that single-stage pure RL maintains higher policy entropy than SFT-initialized RL throughout training. This observation suggests a possible role for exploration in the performance gains observed under unified image-video training. Extensive experiments demonstrate that BusterX++ achieves state-of-the-art performance, highlighting the powerful potential of RL for unified cross-modal visual reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[BusterX: MLLM-Powered AI-Generated Video Forgery Detection and Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12620v9)** | 2026-11-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As generative video models become increasingly realistic, detecting AI-generated videos requires systems that offer both accuracy and interpretability. However, applying Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to video forensics is currently limited by outdated datasets, simplistic evaluation protocols, and a reliance on black-box classification. To address these issues, we introduce a comprehensive dataset, benchmark, and baseline model for video forgery detection. First, we present \textbf{GenBuster-200K}, a fair dataset of over 200,000 high-quality videos sourced from state-of-the-art generators, featuring diverse real-world scenarios. Instead of direct binary classification, BusterX formulates detection as a visual reasoning task, where the generated reasoning chain serves as detector itself. Experimental results demonstrate that BusterX outperforms several leading MLLMs in both detection accuracy and rationale quality.</p></details> |  |
| **[HumanSightBench: A Benchmark for Human Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12305v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-based vision-language benchmark datasets, but still struggle to solve the visual questions that people can solve easily. Evaluating the differences between MLLMs and human-level visual perception is challenging due to end-to-end evaluation which combines various skills, or because we are uncertain if individual items are valid. Specifically, observed errors may reflect visual perception, language priors, background knowledge, question ambiguity, and underspecified answers. Our guideline for HumanSightBench is that we construct items that are human-easy by construction and model-hard by evaluation. HumanSightBench contains 2,876 visual questions with short answers across 7 top-level perceptual categories and 21 subcategories. We only include items that pass five inclusion gates: question clarity, answer uniqueness, visual grounding, human solvability, and text-only shortcut filtering. According to the protocol, errors are defined as incorrect answers on human-validated visual tasks with the evaluated interface. The inclusion gates are intended to reduce confounds from ambiguity and external knowledge. The best pass@3 score is 65.6% across eight evaluated MLLMs. No evaluated model exceeds 27.5% on Counting. | **[Let's Think in Two Steps: Mitigating Agreement Bias in MLLMs with Self-Grounded Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11662v4)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Verifiers--functions assigning rewards to agent behavior--have been key to AI progress in math, code, and games. However, extending gains to domains without clear-cut success criteria (e.g., computer use) remains a challenge: while humans can recognize desired outcomes, translating this intuition into scalable rules is nontrivial. Multimodal LLMs (MLLMs) offer a promising solution, given their world knowledge, human-preference alignment, and reasoning skills. We evaluate MLLM verifiers across web navigation, computer use, and robotics, spanning 13 models, 28 judge designs, diverse agent trajectories, and distinct downstream applications. We identify a critical limitation: a strong tendency for MLLMs to over-validate agent behavior, a phenomenon we term agreement bias. This bias is pervasive across models, is resilient to test-time scaling, and harms pipelines relying on MLLM judgments (e.g., self-improvement). We discuss principles for design and evaluation of verifiers, and introduce SGV, a method that improves verification by harnessing MLLMs' own capabilities via modulation of (un)conditional generation. First, an MLLM generates broad priors about desired behavior, independent of the data under evaluation. Then, conditioned on generated priors, it reasons over and judges a candidate trajectory. Our methods yield more human-aligned verifiers, with gains of up to 25pp in failure detection, 14pp in accuracy, and benefits extending to downstream applications. In self-improvement and online supervision, our verifiers boost task completion of a GUI specialist in OSWorld, a diffusion policy in robomimic, and a ReAct agent in VisualWebArena--surpassing the previous SOTA by 20pp. Finally, we release an updated version of VisualWebArena, featuring improved evaluators, high-fidelity environment parallelism with >10x speedups, and VWA-Lite, a 1/3-scale subset with comparable evaluation fidelity.</p></details> | <details><summary>ICLR ...</summary><p>ICLR 2026. Code, models, and data publicly available at https://mshalimay.github.io/agreement-bias-sgv/</p></details> |
| **[Robust Reasoning Segmentation for Open-World MLLMs without Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.16785v2)** | 2026-11-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ensuring trustworthiness and versatility in Open-World Visual Recognition is a core goal of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While integrating segmentation is desirable, prevailing methods require invasive fine-tuning, which often leads to catastrophic forgetting of the model's general-purpose capabilities. This trade-off between task-specific performance and general reliability fundamentally undermines the goal of building unified, trustworthy systems. We introduce LENS (Leveraging kEypoiNts for MLLMs' Segmentation), a novel plug-and-play paradigm designed to equip MLLMs with pixel-level ability without compromising their foundational integrity. LENS attaches a lightweight head to a completely frozen MLLM, interpreting the intrinsic spatial cues embedded in attention maps. It extracts keypoints and translates them into prompt-compatible features for a mask decoder. Extensive experiments validate that LENS achieves segmentation performance competitive with retraining-based methods. Crucially, it largely preserves the MLLM's open-world generalization, avoiding the degradation observed in fine-tuned counterparts. By decoupling segmentation from generation, LENS offers a robust and efficient path toward truly multi-talented and reliable vision models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in International Journal of Computer Vision (IJCV), 2026</p></details> |
| **[TAMEing the Open-World Personalization: Towards Open-Set Personalized MLLM Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14118v1)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personalizing Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) as intelligent assistants has recently emerged as an active research topic, where an MLLM is expected to go beyond generic, one-size-fits-all replies, and instead generate responses grounded in user-specific objects, entities, and concepts encountered during interactions. To address this limitation, we recast conventional MLLM personalization as an Open-Set problem, and propose TAME-O, the first long-context open-set personalized MLLM assistant. Specifically, TAME-O introduces a training-free, plug-and-play Concept Reconciliation Skill (CR Skill). It enables the assistant to automatically unlock novel concepts during prolonged human-MLLM interactions while preventing confusion with previously unlocked ones, allowing the MLLM to continually grasp more and more concepts and progressively refine the modeling of known ones. Extensive experiments under the open-set personalization setting underscore the efficacy of our method.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 Main Track; code and data are available at https://github.com/ronpay/TAME-O</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Proxy Metrics: MLLMs as Human Surrogates for Explainable AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13926v1)** | 2026-11-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Explainable AI (XAI) has expanded into a broad and impactful research area, but the fundamental question of how best to evaluate explanation techniques has proven frustratingly elusive. To do this, we first propose a ``purpose-grounded'' evaluation framework with metrics applicable to both MLLMs and human users alike. Then, we offer some conceptual insights as to why and when we can trust these models to automate this process. Lastly, we conduct extensive empirical testing across five purpose-grounded areas such as model improvement and fourteen popular MLLMs. Results indicate that all models trend positively, but OpenAI and Qwen models generally show higher alignment compared to other variants, which would be the recommended choices in practice. We expect our insights not to replace user testing, but rather to help XAI researchers complement their current evaluation methodologies and move the field away from heavy reliance on proxy metrics for measuring explanation utility.</p></details> | <details><summary>Neura...</summary><p>Neural Information Processing Systems. NeurIPS 2026. Sydney</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Modality Preference: Controlling Multimodal Arbitration under Modality Conflict in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12883v1)** | 2026-11-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) integrate information across modalities, but become unreliable when those modalities disagree. Building on this, we introduce a lightweight, training-free intervention that biases these heads toward a designated trusted modality at inference time. | **[Conceal, Reconstruct, Jailbreak: Exploiting the Reconstruction-Concealment Tradeoff in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05709v2)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Intent-obfuscation-based jailbreak attacks on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) transform a harmful query into a concealed multimodal input to bypass safety mechanisms. Through a reconstruction and concealment analysis of representative black-box methods, we find that existing transformations struggle to balance this tradeoff, limiting their effectiveness. In contrast, we show that character-removed variants, constructed by randomly removing characters from the original query, achieve a better balance, motivating their use as the basis for our attack construction. Building on this observation, we propose concealment-aware variant construction, which selects character-removed variants with low harmful keyword/phrase alignment and high mutual diversity, and instantiates them through five prompting strategies that distribute the transformed information across text and image channels. We further introduce keyword/phrase-related distractor images that depict the harmful concept in diverse contexts, providing more effective distraction than generic distractor images. Code and reproduction instructions are available at https://github.com/Farhamdur/CRJ.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 camera-ready version; 43 pages, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[Beyond RGB: Benchmarking and Enhancing MLLMs for Hyperspectral Image Understanding via Training-Free Reasoning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08884v3)** | 2026-11-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on RGB image understanding, yet their ability to use spectral evidence beyond the visible range remains largely unexplored. | **[One Agent to Guide Them All: Empowering MLLMs for Vision-and-Language Navigation via Explicit World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15400v2)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A navigation agent must understand high-level semantic instructions and reason about precise spatial structure. | **[Fundus-R1: Training a Fundus-Reading MLLM on Public Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08322v3)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Fundus imaging such as CFP, OCT and SLO is crucial for the early detection of retinal anomalies and diseases. | **[MaRiO: Multi-agent Collaborative Reasoning via Shared Observations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10344v1)** | 2026-11-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show strong visual perception capabilities, but existing evaluations largely focus on single-view or multi-view settings with shared camera parameters, leaving the challenge of multi-agent collaborative scenarios underexplored. To study this problem, we introduce MaRiO, a benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaborative reasoning, and evaluate 23 state-of-the-art MLLMs on it. Our analysis reveals three key findings: (1) tasks governed by static perception are generally easier than tasks that require transformational event sequences; (2) most models struggle to resolve cross-agent correspondences, with errors increasing with scene complexity; and (3) models show limited ability to leverage geometric cues present in the scene. As an initial step toward addressing these challenges, we explore geometry-guided synthetic view augmentation that generates intermediate transition frames between agent observations, providing auxiliary spatial context that improves performance in certain categories. Overall, our results highlight that multi-agent collaborative reasoning remains an open challenge and an important direction for multimodal embodied systems. First two authors contributed equally</p></details> |
| **[Read It Back: Pretrained MLLMs Are Zero-Shot Reward Models for Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11886v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose SpectraReward, a training-free reward function that turns pretrained MLLMs into off-the-shelf reward models for image-generation reinforcement learning. | **[OpenView: Empowering MLLMs with Out-of-view VQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.18563v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show great potential in natural image understanding. Experimental results show that despite having a large gap from human performance in OOV VQA answer selection, multiple MLLMs, upon empowered by OpenView, can consistently boost their performance, uplifted from 48.3% to 62.3% on average. Code, benchmark, and data are available at https://github.com/q1xiangchen/OpenView.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Code: https://github.com/q1xiangchen/OpenView</p></details> |

Second, we are the first to take a decoupled view of multi-modal (MM) understanding and language modeling abilities. Extensive experiments across various benchmarks, covering models with 2B-8B parameters, show that ProQuant significantly outperforms existing methods. For example, our W4A8 Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct improves average accuracy by impressively 6.6% and achieves performance comparable to full precision counterparts with only approximately 1% performance drop. | **[PSGAgent: Enhancing MLLMs' Dynamic Visual Perception for Panoptic Scene Graph Generation via Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07408v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Panoptic Scene Graph generation (PSG) requires holistic yet fine-grained reasoning over complex relations among objects. PSGAgent allows MLLMs to dynamically refine their visual perception using two tools: a Region Zoom-In Tool for local exploration of small or boundary relations and a Visual Highlight Tool for relation-focused segmentation that filters out irrelevant contextual noise. To optimize decision-making, we construct a two-stage training pipeline. First, we create a high-quality, error-aware Multimodal Chain-of-Thought (M-CoT) dataset that provides robust supervision for cold-start learning. Then, we design a novel hierarchical reward mechanism integrated with Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO) to enable strategic and adaptive tool usage. Extensive experiments on the PSG benchmark show that PSGAgent achieves SOTA performance, demonstrating strong capability in both holistic and fine-grained scene understanding.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ACM Multimedia 2026. 10 pages, 8 figures</p></details> |
| **[Breaking Bad Molecules: Are MLLMs Ready for Structure-Level Molecular Detoxification?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.10912v5)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Toxicity remains a leading cause of early-stage drug development failure. We systematically assess 43 mainstream general-purpose MLLMs and conduct multiple ablation studies to analyze key issues, including evaluation metrics, candidate diversity, and failure attribution.
