| **[Where to Connect? Boosting MLLMs via Dynamic Gated Pathways across ALL ViT and LLM Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06844v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A central yet under-explored question in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) is where to connect---how visual information from a hierarchical vision encoder should be wired into the layer-wise semantics of a large language model. We address this gap with DGP (Dynamic Gated Pathways), a routing mechanism that establishes all-to-all connections between every ViT layer and every LLM layer, with each LLM layer dynamically gating its preference over visual layers conditioned on its own semantic state. Accepted at the 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Learning to Group and Order: Cross-Instance Self-Supervised RL for Vision-Centric MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00892v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training has become an important paradigm for improving multimodal large language models (MLLMs), especially with automatically verifiable rewards. Given two visual instances, GO-SSL mixes their local elements and trains the model to group them by instance of origin while recovering the order within each group, coupling instance-level comparison with spatial, temporal, or geometric structure recovery. This paired formulation turns the same unlabeled data into richer verifiable supervision through diverse pairings, shuffles, and a random-to-hard curriculum. We instantiate GO-SSL mainly on cross-image jigsaw tasks and further extend it to 3D depth and video temporal ordering. Across diverse benchmarks, GO-SSL consistently improves over the base MLLM and prior self-supervised post-training baselines, with clear gains in fine-grained perception and spatial understanding. These results suggest that cross-instance comparison provides a useful self-supervised RL signal for improving transferable visual understanding in MLLMs. Code is available at https://github.com/SEU-VIPGroup/GO-SSL.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Factorized MLLM Embeddings for Composed Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05193v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Composed Image Retrieval (CIR) aims to retrieve a target image given a reference image and a modification text. Rather than directly projecting the composed query into a single embedding without internal reasoning, FactEmbed employs a set of factorized latents that simulate the intended visual modifications via an intermediate target imagination objective. This process encourages the latents to ground into target semantics before forming discriminative representations for retrieval. Specifically, DSRS-Net seamlessly infuses geometric features for deeper reasoning segmentation, without compromising the original multimodal fusion mechanism. This strategy effectively mitigates the modality representation mismatch in MLLMs caused by RGB-only pretraining, while enhancing the integration of geometric, contextual, and linguistic features. | **[VideoMemBench: How Well Do MLLMs Remember in Video Conversations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40131v3)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can now process hour-long videos, making extended multi-turn dialogue over a single video a natural mode of use. Sustaining such a dialogue requires more than answering each question in isolation: the model must retrieve and reuse information established earlier in the conversation, both what it has seen and what it has said, an ability we term in-context video memory. We introduce VideoMemBench, in which each sample pairs a single video with a long multi-turn dialogue, whose turns are connected by typed cross-turn dependencies: an Episodic Dependency refers back to a visual episode described in an earlier turn, probing memory of what the model has seen, while an Answer Dependency builds on the model's own earlier answer, probing memory of what the model has said. Every dependency is verified considering two properties: 1) necessity, meaning the question cannot be answered without its earlier turns, and 2) non-leakage, meaning the question does not restate the information from its earlier turns. To enforce these properties, we design a construction pipeline with three LLM roles: a planner, a generator, and a judger, with each dialogue reviewed by human experts. VideoMemBench comprises 326 videos with 2,476 turns, of which 1,387 depend on earlier turns, including multi-parent dependencies and long-range call-back. Our dataset and evaluation are released.</p></details> |  |
| **[VidVec: Unlocking Video MLLM Embeddings for Video-Text Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08099v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies have adapted generative Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) into embedding extractors for text-vision tasks, typically by fine-tuning them to produce universal representations. Our method uses dense video captions as rich textual surrogates for video semantics and short summaries as compact, query-like views, showing that a carefully designed text-alignment objective can improve multimodal alignment through the shared MLLM backbone. We backup these findings through in-depth analysis. Remarkably, our lightweight text-only optimization outperforms its counterpart trained with paired video-text supervision, which is substantially more computationally expensive. Furthermore, our method outperforms existing approaches, often by a substantial margin, and achieves state-of-the-art performance across common video-text retrieval benchmarks. More broadly, our findings highlight the untapped retrieval capabilities of pretrained MLLMs and establish text-only optimization as a more efficient and effective substitute for large-scale visual supervision.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://iyttor.github.io/VidVec/</p></details> |
| **[NightIQA: A Comprehensive Dataset and Benchmark for MLLM-Based Nighttime Image Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04811v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Nighttime images are inherently susceptible to complex, compound degradations, making accurate quality assessment exceptionally challenging. To bridge this gap, we present NightIQA, a dataset and benchmark suite purpose-built for MLLM-based nighttime IQA. It comprises three core components: NightIQA-Real, a real-world dataset of 1,769 high-resolution images that we captured ourselves with modern equipment, encompassing six canonical distortion types and structured multi-dimensional annotations; NightIQA-Bench, a benchmark of 1,078 visually grounded multiple-choice questions tailored to MLLM-based nighttime IQA, organized around a four-level evaluation hierarchy spanning Perception, Technical Assessment, Localization, and Reasoning; and NightIQA-Syn, an inherently scalable, scene-aware synthesis pipeline that jointly models global-level and instance-level degradations, with an initial release of 80k high-fidelity images alongside the full generation code. | **[Sketch-Omni: Instance-Level Sketch-Based Visual Prompting for Perception MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04262v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Promptable perception, from GroundingDINO to Rex-Omni, puts one model behind one prompt interface and returns coordinates for any localisation task. On a sketch, the sketch is the image, and text prompts detect, point, resolve object and spatial referring expressions, and ground affordances in it. From a sketch, the sketch is the prompt, and a drawing of one object localises that object in a photograph, even among several of the same category, a query that neither a category name nor a photo exemplar can pose. Across both models and four pruning methods, Raincheck recovers on average 77.7% and 60.4% of the quality lost to matched hard pruning on MultiVerse and ConvBench, respectively. Our results quantitatively demonstrate performance disparities across models and tasks, and highlight a substantial gap between current MLLM capabilities and human expert performance in front-end engineering. The FullFront benchmark and code are available in https://github.com/Mikivishy/FullFront.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decoupling Consistency and Conflict across MLLM Layers for Fake News Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36569v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The trustworthiness of Web content faces a growing threat from multimodal fake news. Real news usually shows aligned image-text semantics, while fake news often contains a few conflicts at varying levels, such as mismatches in objects, scenes, events, and claims. However, existing methods usually treat consistency and conflict in a coarse and static way, limiting their ability to capture sparse conflicts and adapt to varying conflict levels. Inspired by the distinct knowledge encoded across layers in large language models, we propose Multi-Level Consistency and Conflict Decoupling (MLCCD), which captures conflicts at different levels from different layers of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Specifically, we first use the MLLM's final decision representation to select visual and textual tokens relevant to the task, preventing irrelevant content from diluting sparse conflicts. Then, we preserve tokens whose representations change sharply between adjacent layers, capturing the knowledge each layer adds. Finally, we decouple this knowledge into consistency and conflict evidence and aggregate each across layers with its own router, adapting to varying conflict levels. Experiments on three benchmarks show that MLCCD outperforms existing methods.</p></details> | 12 pages |
| **[MulFCoder: Framework-conditioned Multi-agent for MLLM-based Multi-framework Front-end Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34418v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generating runnable front-end code from UI screenshots is a long-standing goal in automated software engineering. These findings indicate that scaling passive multi-view perception does not intrinsically yield active spatial reasoning, highlighting the need for models that know where to look, how to integrate what they see, and when to stop. | **[InteractiveWorld: A Verifiable Human-Centric Environment for Building Better Interactive MLLM Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33966v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>People have long dreamed of an assistant that is always there to guide them and remind what they forget, from new dishes to lost keys. | **[Rescuing Your Pruning: Rethinking Visual Token Pruning and Post-Training Recovery in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33688v1)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual token pruning substantially reduces the inference cost of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), yet existing methods are mostly training-free and plug-and-play. For joint compression, we find that the vision encoder, not the LLM, dominates prefill time in native-resolution MLLMs, so where tokens are removed matters more than how many: keeping 40% of tokens at the encoder runs faster than keeping 10% at the projector. Our best configuration prunes the encoder lightly and the projector aggressively, leaves the LLM intact, and keeps general understanding and reasoning at the dense level. It accelerates the LLM stage by 3.49x but the end-to-end time-to-first-token by only 1.54x, showing that LLM-stage speedups overstate end-to-end gains. | **[TerraVis: Towards Evaluation of World-Grounded Visual Consistency in Text-to-Image Generation via MLLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02959v2)** | 2026-10-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent text-to-image models have made substantial progress in photorealism, aesthetics, and text-image alignment. We introduce StemBind, a shared-stem diagnostic benchmark that probes the same visual stem with three aligned questions: Perception (what is in the image), Rule (what pattern governs it), and Full (which option completes it), so a final-answer error can be attributed to a specific sub-step on the same evidence. (ii) A persistent binding gap: even when P and R are both correct on the same stem, models still answer F incorrectly 51.2% of the time. (iii) The bottleneck is S3: process diagnostics and Stage-wise Stimulus Augmentation localize the dominant failure to rule-to-instance mapping. (iv) Scaling and thinking do not help: neither larger models nor explicit thinking mode reliably closes the gap, and thinking even lowers rule and full-item accuracy. Third, for temporal inference, text-only and text-plus-complete-video observations share a video target, and the trained DiT is then frozen while the MLLM and connector are optimized through it. This final stage trains limited-input representations for generation while retaining complete-observation reconstruction as a complementary training mode. | **[VENOMREC: Cross-Modal Interactive Poisoning for Targeted Promotion in Multimodal LLM Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06409v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are pushing recommender systems (RecSys) toward content-grounded retrieval and ranking via cross-modal fusion. We find that while cross-modal consensus often mitigates conventional poisoning that manipulates interaction logs or perturbs a single modality, it also introduces a new attack surface where synchronised multimodal poisoning can reliably steer fused representations along stable semantic directions during fine-tuning. To characterise this threat, we formalise cross-modal interactive poisoning and propose VENOMREC, which performs Exposure Alignment to identify high-exposure regions in the joint embedding space and Cross-modal Interactive Perturbation to craft attention-guided coupled token--patch edits. Experiments on four real-world multimodal datasets demonstrate that VENOMREC consistently outperforms strong baselines, achieving 0.73 mean ER@20 and improving over the strongest baseline by +0.52 absolute ER points on average, while maintaining comparable recommendation utility. Code is available at https://github.com/GuoweiGuan666/VenomRec.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ICML 2026</p></details> |
| **[C3H: Compression-to-Consensus Criteria Hijacking in Multimodal LLM Recommender Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.32780v1)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are transforming recommender systems into content-aware decision systems. In these systems, multimodal compression and cross-modal fusion are often viewed as robustness-enhancing mechanisms against content manipulation, as compression can filter noisy signals and cross-modal consensus can suppress inconsistent anomalies. We challenge this assumption by showing that the same process can expose a compression-to-consensus (C2C) bias in ranking decisions. Signals that remain salient after compression and receive consistent support across modalities can gain disproportionate influence in candidate comparison, offering an exploitable direction for content manipulation. In this paper, we introduce C3H, a novel inference-time attack that exploits this vulnerability. Rather than relying on noisy perturbations or training-time poisoning, C3H promotes a target item by tailoring its multimodal content to align with the decision criteria implicitly expressed by the recommender. Extensive experiments demonstrate that C3H substantially increases target-item exposure while maintaining overall recommendation utility. Code is available at https://github.com/GuoweiGuan666/C3H.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[SIEVES: Selective Prediction Generalizes through Visual Evidence Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.25855v3)** | 2026-11-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As the accuracy of Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on traditional visual question answering benchmarks approaches saturation, reliable deployment requires satisfying low error tolerances in real-world out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios. Selective prediction aims to improve coverage, i.e. the share of inputs the system answers, while adhering to a user-defined risk level. This is typically achieved by assigning a confidence score to each answer and abstaining on those that fall below a certain threshold. Existing selective prediction methods rely on text-only reasoning and learn to predict confidences simply based on whether the answer was correct or not. In contrast, we introduce SIEVES (Selective Prediction through Visual Evidence Scoring), which first requires the answering model to provide visual evidence supporting its answer, by returning crops of the image alongside their textual reasoning and answer. Second, a lightweight selector is trained to predict the quality of this evidence, assessing how well the provided crops localize the relevant visual elements and if the final answer is coherent with this evidence. We show that SIEVES improves coverage by up to six times on challenging OOD benchmarks (V* Bench, HR-Bench-8k, MME-RealWorld-Lite, VizWiz, and AdVQA), compared to non-grounding baselines. | **[Are We Rewarding the Wrong Module? | **[GlanceNav: Efficient Vision-and-Language Navigation with Streaming Memory and Counterfactual Rewind](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30900v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have become strong policies for vision-and-language navigation (VLN). However, most of them encode a sample of past frames again at every decision and are trained on isolated decisions, so computation is redundant and the usable history is short. They also learn from their own mistakes through outcome rewards that give one scalar to a whole episode. We ask whether a navigator can encode each frame only once, at a constant cost per step, and still remember what matters and learn efficiently from its mistakes. We present GlanceNav, a streaming navigator built on a hybrid MLLM. Its Gated DeltaNet layers carry a recurrent state through the whole episode, while its attention layers keep a short window of recent observations and a small bank of keyframes, selected by how much each frame changes the recurrent state. This constant-size memory keeps the cost per step fixed and allows training on whole episodes with the same memory as at inference. Because the entire memory can also be copied in constant time, we further propose Counterfactual Rewind (CoRe), a reinforcement learning stage that rewinds a drifting rollout to its closest approach to the goal, lets an expert correct a few actions, and credits decisions by the resulting change in outcome. Trained only on the R2R-CE and RxR-CE training splits, with 1,000 instructions for reinforcement learning, a 4B GlanceNav reaches success rates of 71.9% and 73.6% on R2R-CE and RxR-CE val-unseen. On R2R-CE, it outperforms the strongest previous single-RGB method trained without external data by 9.1 points, while running at 104 ms per step.</p></details> |  |
| **[WeMM-Ecom: Advancing Multimodal Content Understanding for WeChat E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30662v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present WeMM-Ecom, a family of multimodal large language models that deliver frontier e-commerce capabilities while achieving state-of-the-art general-purpose performance at their respective scales. The family spans five sizes, from a 0.8B dense model to a 35B mixture-of-experts model with 3B active parameters. Our training pipeline begins with two-stage continued pre-training that progressively raises the proportion of e-commerce data within a diverse multimodal data mixture, followed by hybrid-thinking post-training that combines on-policy distillation and reinforcement learning to support both non-thinking and thinking modes. For evaluation, we construct a comprehensive suite of 28 e-commerce benchmarks (23 in-house and five open-source) covering eight business scenarios and complement it with 64 public general-purpose benchmarks. On the e-commerce suite, WeMM-Ecom-35B-A3B surpasses substantially larger open-source flagship models, including Qwen3.8-Flash-Next, HY4, and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash. On the general-purpose suite, every WeMM-Ecom model surpasses its similarly sized Qwen3.5 counterpart, with the lightweight variants (e.g., WeMM-Ecom-2B) showing clear advantages over leading lightweight models such as Qwen3.5-2B, MiniCPM-V-4.7-1B, and LFM3-VL-2B. Beyond benchmarks, WeMM-Ecom now powers hundreds of production use cases in WeChat, including catalog management, multimodal product search, and livestream commerce, serving billion-scale requests per day. We publicly release all model weights and detail our data curation, training recipe, and benchmark construction to facilitate future work on domain-specialized multimodal models. We have released the model weights and code to facilitate future research at https://github.com/Tencent/WeMM-Ecom.</p></details> |  |
| **[Auxiliary Clues Aware's Geometry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30617v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Geometry Problem Solving (GPS) serves as a rigorous test of the complex reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), particularly when solutions require auxiliary constructions absent from the input diagram. However, explicit diagramming methods suffer from unstable image generation quality and cascading errors, while existing implicit attention mechanisms primarily focus on visible elements, limiting their ability to support reasoning that requires additional geometric constructions. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{AuxCA} (Auxiliary-Clues-Aware GRPO), a reinforcement learning framework that helps models learn transferable auxiliary-construction strategies that can be applied beyond the training domain without explicit diagram generation at inference time. Specifically, we introduce Auxiliary Clues Dependency (\textbf{ACD}) to quantify the influence of auxiliary visual clues on token generation and integrate it into GRPO through fine-grained advantage weighting. Furthermore, we incorporate an auxiliary incentive reward to encourage the effective use of auxiliary geometric clues in correct solutions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AuxCA-8B outperforms the explicit-plotting baseline Bagel-Canvas and achieves competitive performance with leading proprietary models on auxiliary-dependent plane geometry problems. Improvements on analytic geometry, solid geometry, and external benchmarks further suggest that the learned strategies can transfer beyond the training domain. Code is available at https://github.com/fukua23/AuxCA</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS2026</p></details> |
| **[Test-Time Latent Computation to Improve Visual Perception in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30480v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work on Large Language Models (LLMs) suggests that reasoning capabilities can be enhanced at test time not only through longer token-level reasoning traces, but also through additional computation in latent space. This raises a natural question for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs): can additional computation in latent space improve visual perception at test time? In this work, we first verify that MLLMs contain stable reusable layers that can be re-forwarded at inference time without disrupting normal generation. Based on this observation, we propose \textbf{Lo}ok \textbf{Ba}ck in the \textbf{L}atent \textbf{S}pace (\textbf{Lobals}), a training-free decoding method that identifies the contiguous layer window with the strongest visual-token attention and re-forwards through it, allowing the model to revisit visual evidence in latent space. We demonstrate that this latent-space revisit strategy can improve MLLMs' performance across diverse multimodal settings, including general vision-language tasks, visual hallucination tasks, and vision-centric tasks.</p></details> |  |
| **[Rethinking the Evaluation of Contrastive Decoding for Hallucination Mitigation in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30460v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contrastive decoding (CD) mitigates object hallucination in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) by contrasting token distributions under different conditions to adjust token scores. However, existing generative and discriminative benchmarks primarily measure final task scores, which can be confounded by candidate filtering, changes in generation trajectories, and response biases, making it difficult to isolate the effects of the underlying contrastive adjustments. To address these limitations while complementing existing evaluations, we introduce CONCOR (Contrastive Correction Evaluation), a controlled benchmark that directly evaluates the competition between ground-truth and different types of hallucinated candidates using the Contrastive Grounding Margin (CGM). CGM also enables analysis of directional consistency, composition, and reliability of contrastive corrections. Our evaluation reveals that existing CD methods exhibit distinct correction behaviors: some consistently favor ground-truth candidates, whereas others produce strong corrections but also introduce more errors. Crucially, these differences help explain their contrasting responses to increased contrast strength, which improves some methods while degrading others. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating the contrastive correction itself, rather than relying solely on final-output performance, and CONCOR provides a controlled and interpretable framework for understanding and improving CD methods for hallucination mitigation.</p></details> |  |
| **[TamperCert: A Benchmark for Detecting Text Tampering in Degree Certificates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30419v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Degree certificates play a critical role in high-stakes decisions such as university admission, immigration, and hiring. The issuing institution vouches for the holder, so a forged certificate may let its bearer pass these checks. Degree certificate forgery is increasingly prevalent. Yet, many assume that it is easy to spot. Reported cases show otherwise: in 2024, the business school of the University of Hong Kong found that about 30 master's students had enrolled with forged academic credentials. Few studies have looked at how to detect forged degree certificates, partly because public benchmarks are scarce. We present TamperCert, a benchmark built from 400 real degree certificates issued by 38 universities. Each certificate was subjected to 11 publicly available tampering methods to alter the holder's name and further processed with four additional post-processing techniques, resulting in over 20,000 forged images. We evaluate 13 detectors, a commercial multimodal large language model (MLLM), and detectors trained on TamperCert.Our findings reveal that no detector is reliable for every tampering method. Consequently, a determined forger may still evade detection. Moreover, the commercial MLLM misses most forgeries made by general-purpose generative models, and post-processing, such as resizing an image or photographing it with a camera, severely degrades detection.</p></details> |  |
| **[Face Is Not All You Need: MIME Benchmark for Incomplete Multimodal Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30288v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Emotion Recognition (MER) is a fundamental task in multimedia understanding, where state-of-the-art methods have achieved prominent success relying on the assumption of complete, well-aligned multimodal inputs. However, real-world unconstrained scenarios often suffer from unpredictable modality degradation and information loss, leading to catastrophic performance collapse of existing MER models that heavily depend on facial cues as the core inference anchor. However, few systematic works evaluate model robustness under diverse, fine-grained incomplete settings, as prior works are limited to coarse-grained full-modality removal and neglect interpretable analysis of emotional reasoning. To bridge this gap, we formally define the Incomplete Multimodal Emotion Recognition (IMER) task, which benchmarks model generalization and robustness under multi-grained modality and emotional information loss. We further construct MIME, a dedicated IMER benchmark with 2,000 video segments covering realistic fine-grained facial-detail degradation, severe facial degradation, and extreme full-modality loss, surpassing the limited coarse-grained settings of prior works. We also propose the Chain of Emotion (CoE) analysis paradigm with tailored metrics to dissect the fine-grained emotional cue perception and reasoning process of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Extensive experiments on seven mainstream MLLMs reveal that body language and scene context act as critical compensatory mechanisms under facial degradation, while a pronounced gap exists between models' basic visual perception ability and high-level cognitive reasoning. Our work provides a focused evaluation platform and actionable insights for advancing robust multimodal emotion recognition. Our dataset and code are public at https://yuxinokk.github.io/MIME.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published in the Proceedings of the 34th ACM International Conference on Multimedia (ACM MM '26), November 10--14, 2026, Rio de Janeiro, Brazil</p></details> |
| **[TriSegAgent: Context-Efficient 3D Annotation with MLLMs via Tri-Planes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30240v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive segmentation models turn a few clicks into a segmentation prediction across domains, so annotators only decide where the prediction still needs correction. Annotation thus becomes a loop of inspecting the prediction and clicking again. Each step of this loop is a visual judgment, which suits general-purpose multimodal large language model (MLLM) agents. In this work, we focus on letting these agents run this loop on 3D volumes. However, the MLLM in such an agent takes only 2D images as input, so the agent must observe the 3D volume in 2D. Since the loop calls the MLLM many times, this observation must be token-compact enough to keep every call cheap, yet informative enough to show error regions in the 3D prediction. We meet both needs with tri-planes, the three orthogonal planes that cross at one point in the volume. We show, both theoretically and empirically, that tri-planes are context-efficient yet keep sufficient detail to show error regions in the 3D prediction. Building on tri-planes, we introduce TriSegAgent, an MLLM agent that refines the 3D prediction iteratively. Within the agent, the MLLM only judges the prediction on tri-planes, while a visual harness turns each judgment into a 3D action in code. To make the loop reliable, the harness places each click at an exact voxel in the volume and checks whether to accept the 3D prediction. To evaluate the agent, we build a diverse benchmark of 690 regions of interest (ROIs) from 35 public medical datasets across three imaging modalities. With frontier MLLMs, TriSegAgent reaches an overall Dice about 10 points above the strongest baseline, and its lead is even larger on targets below 3 cm. With flash-level MLLMs, it outperforms a recent 3D segmentation agent built on a frontier MLLM.</p></details> |  |
| **[PhysAgent: A Multi-Agent Framework for Reliable Remote Heart Rate Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00066v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Remote photoplethysmography (rPPG) enables non-contact heart-rate estimation from facial videos, but its weak physiological signal is easily corrupted by motion, illumination changes, occlusion, skin-appearance variation, and device noise. Existing rPPG methods typically rely on a single model to directly predict heart rate or recover pulse waveforms, while different strong estimators may produce conflicting yet individually plausible candidates for the same video. To resolve these conflicts, we propose PhysAgent, an inference-time multi-agent candidate-verification framework. Unlike direct prediction approaches, PhysAgent neither trains a new base rPPG model nor asks Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to output heart rate directly. In contrast, it treats outputs from multiple base estimators as physiological hypotheses to be verified and uses a lightweight 4B MLLM, Qwen3-VL-4B, to drive multi-agent reasoning over video conditions, signal reliability, and candidate disagreement. A deterministic physiological verifier checks the fusion proposal, and a reproducible numerical fusion process produces the final heart rate. Experimental results on multiple public rPPG benchmarks show that PhysAgent improves fusion stability and reliability across different datasets and source-domain settings, while avoiding the irreproducibility and physiological inconsistency of direct MLLM prediction or unconstrained ensemble fusion. The code will be released soon.</p></details> |  |
| **[OmniSenMo: A Unified Multisensory Language Model for Generalizable Human Motion Estimation, Understanding, and Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.30176v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) estimate, understand, and predict human motion, but mainly from RGB cameras. Depth, LiDAR, mmWave, and WiFi complement RGB under poor lighting, through occlusions, and in privacy-sensitive applications, but existing multisensory models cover only some of these sensing tasks. Covering all of them from any sensor subset is challenging, since the tokens of heterogeneous sensors are semantically inconsistent and spatially misaligned, paired multisensory data are scarce, and the tasks need different temporal granularities. We present OmniSenMo, a multisensory language model that takes the human skeleton as a common reference for all sensors. Its skeleton-grounded motion representation turns each sensor into joint tokens, one per body joint, so that any sensor subset is fused joint by joint and expressed in the motion vocabulary that the language model learned from motion capture and text. We pretrain it on all five sensors generated by our multisensory simulator, and Multi-Granularity Observation gives each task the frames it needs. On a cross-action split of MM-Fi, the same model with all five sensors outperforms the models compared on each task, including LEO-Fuse in pose estimation (84.1 vs. 89.0 mm MPJPE) and HoloLLM in unseen-action recognition (69.2% vs. 42.8%). Even without RGB, it outperforms both methods that use RGB, and in a cross-dataset validation on XRF55, it recognizes unseen actions far better than HoloLLM.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://ch3ngguo.github.io/omnisenmo/</p></details> |
| **[GlyphWeaver: Unlocking Glyph Design Creativity with Uniform Glyph DSL and AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08444v3)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Expressive glyph visualizations provide a powerful and versatile means to represent complex multivariate data through compact visual encodings, but creating custom glyphs remains challenging due to the gap between design creativity and technical implementation. We present GlyphWeaver, a novel interactive system to enable an easy creation of expressive glyph visualizations. Our system comprises three key components: a glyph domain-specific language (GDSL), a GDSL operation management mechanism, and a multimodal interaction interface. The GDSL is a hierarchical container model, where each container is independent and composable, providing a rigorous yet practical foundation for complex glyph visualizations. The operation management mechanism restricts modifications of the GDSL to atomic operations, making it accessible without requiring direct coding. The multimodal interaction interface enables direct manipulation, natural language commands, and parameter adjustments. A multimodal large language model acts as a translator, converting these inputs into GDSL operations. GlyphWeaver significantly lowers the barrier for designers, who often do not have extensive programming skills, to create sophisticated glyph visualizations.</p></details> |  |
| **[EgoCaptioner: Towards Comprehensive and Fine-Grained Egocentric Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26380v2)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric video captioning requires fine-grained detail and accurate timing, yet current multimodal large language models (MLLMs) often miss brief actions, confuse their order, or misinterpret the wearer's motion. Complementary rewards assess factual accuracy and coverage, measure step-level temporal alignment, and penalize fragmented or redundant narration, guiding caption refinement through Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). To improve the reliability of factual supervision, we supplement caption references with pose-derived motion facts when sensor data are available. Across five egocentric benchmarks, EgoCaptioner achieves leading performance among evaluated open-source models in caption quality, caption-conditioned question answering, and temporal alignment. It also surpasses Gemini 3.1 Pro on PhysCapBench and all evaluated proprietary baselines on Minerva-Ego under the caption-to-QA protocol. Ablations support the complementary roles of factual and temporal rewards and show that coarse-to-fine annotation provides more effective supervision than single-pass annotation.</p></details> | 44 pages |
| **[Cross-modal RAG: Sub-dimensional Text-to-Image Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21956v4)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Text-to-image generation increasingly demands access to domain-specific, fine-grained, and rapidly evolving knowledge that pretrained models cannot fully capture, necessitating the integration of retrieval methods. We propose Cross-modal RAG, a novel framework that decomposes both queries and images into sub-dimensional components, enabling subquery-aware retrieval and generation. Our method introduces a hybrid retrieval strategy, combining a sub-dimensional sparse retriever with a dense retriever, to identify a Pareto-optimal set of images, each contributing complementary aspects of the query. During generation, a multimodal large language model is guided to selectively condition on relevant visual features aligned to specific subqueries for subquery-aware image synthesis. | **[Divide and Conquer: A Hybrid Strategy Defeats Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16555v6)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are widely applied in various fields of society due to their powerful reasoning, understanding, and generation capabilities. However, the security issues associated with these models are becoming increasingly severe. Jailbreaking attacks, as an important method for detecting vulnerabilities in LLMs, have been explored by researchers who attempt to induce these models to generate harmful content through various attack methods. Nevertheless, existing jailbreaking methods face numerous limitations, such as excessive query counts, limited coverage of jailbreak modalities, low attack success rates, and simplistic evaluation methods. To overcome these constraints, this paper proposes a multimodal jailbreaking method: JMLLM. This method integrates multiple strategies to perform comprehensive jailbreak attacks across text, visual, and auditory modalities. Additionally, we contribute a new and comprehensive dataset for multimodal jailbreaking research: TriJail, which includes jailbreak prompts for all three modalities. Experiments on the TriJail dataset and the benchmark dataset AdvBench, conducted on 13 popular LLMs, demonstrate advanced attack success rates and significant reduction in time overhead.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Language Optimization: Enhancing Visual Representations for Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29783v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Accurate chest X-ray radiology report generation (RRG) requires recognizing clinically relevant abnormalities and accurately describing them. Although Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) improves the clinical accuracy of generated reports, our analyses suggest limited improvements in visual representations for abnormality recognition. To address this limitation, we propose a selective regional visual distillation framework that enhances visual representations of challenging abnormalities while preserving existing visual capabilities. Specifically, we train a region-aware teacher with targeted supervision on challenging anatomical regions to acquire complementary visual knowledge. Rather than transferring all teacher features, we verify their utility through regional feature replacement and select only those that improve the original student's performance. We further introduce a Spatially Gated Residual Adapter (SGRA) to distill these verified features while minimizing interference with the student's existing visual representations. Experiments on MIMIC-CXR and IU-Xray demonstrate improved clinical report accuracy and region-level abnormality recognition across different multimodal large language model backbones, while largely preserving performance on non-target findings.</p></details> |  |
| **[S1Arena-MM: Which Multimodal Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29773v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Perception systems often make discrete decisions: flag a hateful meme, predict whether a pedestrian will cross, detect a spoofed voice, or select the next move in a game. System-1 models, such as OneJev and Jev-Omni, read the media and return probabilities over the options in a single forward pass, while multimodal large language models (MLLMs) generate text. Which multimodal decisions can System-1 models handle, and where do MLLMs perform better? We introduce S1Arena-MM, a benchmark that gives every model the same decision requests and scores the probabilities it returns. It contains 51,060 questions across 94 tasks with image, video, and audio input, drawn from 94 public datasets and three program-generated ones. It also includes 102 closed-loop game environments that a model plays from screenshots; in real-time mode, the game continues while the model decides. We evaluate 33 open models: 12 System-1 models and 21 MLLMs read at the answer token. On static decisions, the best System-1 model leads the best MLLM by 4.9 percentage points on images and is within one point of it on video and audio. Post-training for decisions mainly improves the probabilities: on video, a System-1 model has a lower Brier score than the MLLM that it is post-trained from in 10 of 12 pairs. With up to 2,048 thought tokens per question, the most accurate MLLMs pass the best System-1 models only slightly, and thinking raises the Brier score of most MLLMs. Closed loops separate the models: three System-1 models built on a 27B MLLM reach 22.0-33.3% progression in BALROG, and no other model exceeds 9.7% with thinking off. In real-time GameWorld, latencies of up to 1.1 s per decision cause no measurable loss of progress for System-1 models. Code and data: https://github.com/zhaoyang02/S1Arena-MM.</p></details> | <details><summary>26 pa...</summary><p>26 pages. Lang Lin, Tianyu Chen, Yi Gu, Yang Zhao, Xinran Song and Xinyang Liu contributed equally. Code and data: https://github.com/zhaoyang02/S1Arena-MM ; media: https://huggingface.co/datasets/Swindl/S1Arena-MM</p></details> |
| **[Inspect Before You Decide: Evidence-Guided Visual Search for Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29551v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable potential for industrial anomaly detection and understanding. However, reliably identifying subtle anomalies remains challenging, as existing approaches often struggle to acquire and verify fine-grained visual evidence beyond global observations. To address this challenge, we propose EVAD, an evidence-guided visual search framework that enables MLLMs to actively seek and examine diagnostically informative visual evidence for reliable anomaly reasoning. Specifically, EVAD begins with a global visual assessment to identify potentially anomalous regions for further inspection. Building upon this observation, we introduce a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS)-guided exploration strategy that progressively acquires visual evidence through two complementary operations: semantic refinement for fine-grained inspection and spatial expansion for contextual verification. The acquired evidence is subsequently evaluated and integrated to support more informed and reliable anomaly decisions. Extensive experiments across multiple industrial anomaly detection tasks demonstrate that EVAD achieves state-of-the-art performance in most evaluated settings, highlighting the effectiveness of active evidence exploration for fine-grained anomaly understanding and reasoning. | **[CAR: Reducing Decoder Output Shift in Visual Token Pruning via Coverage Selection and Attention Reallocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29489v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Visual token pruning is an effective way to accelerate multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing methods degrade substantially under aggressive pruning, especially on visual grounding (VG). We analyze the sources of this degradation from the perspective of the output shift that pruning causes in decoder layers, and decompose this shift in closed form into two terms, an image summary error and an attention allocation error. The summary error stems from the loss of visual information, and the allocation error from the drop in the share of attention assigned to visual tokens. To reduce both errors, we propose CAR (Coverage selection and Attention Reallocation), a token pruning framework that restores attention share while maintaining information. Specifically, to minimize the summary error, whose upper bound tightens as the retained tokens better cover all visual tokens, our coverage selection retains tokens that are both salient and non-redundant, with saliency from encoder heads chosen by a label-free probe. To minimize the allocation error, our attention reallocation reassigns the attention of the discarded tokens to the retained ones through a closed-form logit bias. On Qwen2.5-VL-7B with $90\%$ of the visual tokens pruned, CAR retains $93.0\%$ of the unpruned accuracy on visual question answering (VQA) and $77.9\%$ on VG, against $26.5\%$ for the best existing method on VG; the logit bias alone raises the VG accuracy of existing methods by up to $24$ points. | **[Remote-Sensing City Layout Extraction with MLLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16484v2)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Remote-sensing systems usually describe urban content with detection boxes, semantic masks, or vector boundaries. Such outputs locate classes and support image-plane scoring, yet they do not by themselves constitute an executable layout that retains object identities, typed relations, topology, and regeneration rules. An image model first produces an aligned five-class semantic layout prior. Three ordered MLLM passes use the image and this prior to recover roads, land-cover regions and relations, and buildings. Deterministic normalization converts the accumulated records into a city graph and a restricted layout program. Executing the program creates a renderable 3D city layout and an orthographic semantic projection over shared geometry. The projection admits pixel-level comparison with remote-sensing masks, while named objects, relations, and editing operations remain available for synchronized regeneration of both views. Evaluated on the 100 scenes of CityLayout-100, the complete framework obtains 41.1% mean intersection-over-union and 48.3% global intersection-over-union. This result provides quantitative evidence that visual observations can be translated into inspectable, editable city code with coupled planar and 3D outputs.</p></details> | <details><summary>4 pag...</summary><p>4 pages, 2 figures, 4 tables. Accepted to IEEE APGARSS 2026</p></details> |
| **[CT-Navigator: Towards Interpretable and Generalizable CT Understanding through Agentic Perception and Tool-Invocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29305v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical computed tomography (CT) interpretation inherently requires progressive visual exploration, where radiologists navigate from whole scans to individual slices and regions of interest and interact with external tools when necessary. However, existing 3D medical multimodal large language models (MLLMs) typically connect a specialized 3D visual encoder to a language model, without fully leveraging the native 2D visual and agentic capabilities of generalist MLLMs. Trained on large-scale one-hop CT instruction-following data, these models also have limited ability to perform iterative reasoning and explicitly ground their conclusions in visual evidence, limiting their support for traceable CT interpretation. To solve these problems, we propose \textbf{agentic perception} as an alternative paradigm inspired by radiologists' interpretation workflows. We introduce \textbf{CT-Navigator}, a framework that harnesses generalist MLLMs' 2D visual and agentic capabilities to progressively ground CT interpretation in explicit, traceable visual evidence through hierarchical visual exploration and radiology tool invocation. To build these capabilities, we curate 8,904 visual chain-of-thought (V-CoT) trajectories for cold-start agentic supervised fine-tuning. We then optimize the policy through reinforcement learning on 33,219 questions, with data selection and dynamic rollout sampling prioritizing informative learning signals. Finally, we introduce CTNavi-Bench, a 3D CT visual question answering benchmark comprising 7,000 questions spanning anatomical regions throughout the human body. CT-Navigator achieves 79.50\% accuracy on CTNavi-Bench, surpassing the strongest baseline by 17.59\%, and demonstrates strong generalization across three external CT benchmarks. The code, model, and benchmark will be released on \href{https://github.com/fyb99/ct_navigator}{GitHub}.</p></details> |  |
| **[Toward Cognitive Supersensing in Multimodal Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01541v2)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made rapid progress in open-vocabulary visual understanding, yet they often struggle with cognitive visual reasoning tasks that require maintaining, transforming, and simulating abstract visual states. Existing approaches primarily scale Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in language, forcing inherently spatial or visual computations into discrete text tokens. We introduce Cognitive Supersensing, a training paradigm that equips MLLMs with latent visual reasoning by integrating a Latent Visual Imagery Prediction (LVIP) head that progressively learns refined visual latent states aligned with the evolving reasoning context and target answer, thereby providing a structured visual grounding signal for internal reasoning. We further introduce a reinforcement learning stage that optimizes rationale trajectories using both answer evidence and LVIP-based latent grounding, encouraging reasoning paths that remain visually consistent. To evaluate the cognitive capabilities of MLLMs, we present CogSense-Bench, a comprehensive visual question answering (VQA) benchmark assessing five cognitive dimensions. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MLLMs trained with Cognitive Supersensing significantly outperform state-of-the-art baselines on CogSense-Bench and exhibit superior generalization on out-of-domain mathematics and science VQA benchmarks, suggesting that internal visual imagery may be key to bridging the gap between perceptual recognition and cognitive understanding. Causal analyses through noise injection, latent patching, and linear probing show that LVIP states progressively encode task-relevant visual structure and influence downstream answer probabilities, supporting their role as active reasoning signals.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Learning from Another Angle: Rotation-Based Test-Time Training for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28972v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on vision-language tasks but often remain vulnerable to distribution shifts due to their fixed parameters during inference. Extensive experiments across multiple vision-language benchmarks demonstrate that RoTTT consistently improves performance across different MLLMs, providing a practical and efficient paradigm for on-the-fly MLLM adaptation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Let Androids Dream: From Visual Elements to Emergent Implications via Contextual Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17019v3)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image metaphor understanding differs from general VQA: the implied meaning is rarely explicit in pixels and instead emerges from the compositional interaction of visual elements together with social intent and culture-specific background. We then propose Let Androids Dream (LAD), a training-free framework that operationalizes contextual alignment through test-time computation: (1) Perception extracts compositional visual structure, (2) Search adaptively acquires context from parametric knowledge or web search, and (3) Reasoning aligns compositions with context to derive emergent implications. Project page: https://openraiser.github.io/StudioBench/ and code: https://github.com/openraiser/StudioBench . Equal contribution: Xinglong Xu, Yuxia Zhu. We evaluate seven frontier MLLMs on CivilCADBench, and find that they read and redraw printed content accurately but recover the file structure poorly: the best model scores above 90% in understanding and single-page reconstruction, yet only 60% on layer assignment and 48% on design. | **[HighwayVerse: A Chain-of-Inspection Benchmark for Highway Infrastructure Anomaly Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28800v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Practical highway infrastructure inspection entails progressive diagnostic reasoning from visual perception to maintenance decisions, yet existing benchmarks remain confined to isolated tasks, leaving Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) insufficiently evaluated across sequential inspection stages. To address these issues, we propose a multi-view quality assessment method based on a multimodal large language model (MLLM). Such failure reflects two problems: router drift, where expert selection becomes inconsistent over time, and expert drift, where shared experts are overwritten across tasks. Therefore, we propose StAbilized Mixture-of-Experts (SAME) for MCIT. To address router drift, SAME stabilizes expert selection by decomposing routing dynamics into orthogonal subspaces and updating only task-relevant directions. To mitigate expert drift, we regulate expert updates via curvature-aware scaling using historical input covariance in a rehearsal-free manner. SAME also introduces adaptive expert activation to freeze selected experts during training, reducing redundant computation and cross-task interference. | **[FM4SAR-Bench 1.0: Towards A Fair Benchmark for Evaluating Foundation Models for SAR Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.27158v1)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Foundation models have advanced SAR image interpretation but created a heterogeneous, fragmented model ecosystem. Existing models differ substantially in their input, training data, objectives, architectures, and release policy, yet the field lacks a unified taxonomy and comparative framework. Current evaluations rely primarily on task-specific benchmarks, which are ill-suited to fair comparison among models with fundamentally different input-output formats and task paradigms, and often overlook data transparency, reproducibility, interpretability, and deployment readiness. Limited SAR data accessibility also means high reported performance does not guarantee access to training data, code, or weights, limiting reproduction, benchmarking, and deployment. To address these issues, we propose a systematic taxonomy and hierarchical evaluation framework for SAR-related foundation models: five rating levels, six dimensions, and fine-grained criteria, with separate protocols for VFMs and VLMs. Beyond overall ratings, it yields interpretable multidimensional capability profiles and supports user-defined weighting, so rankings follow user priorities. | **[DailySafe: Benchmarking Dynamic Safety Awareness in Everyday Multi-Turn Multimodal Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26298v2)** | 2026-11-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) become increasingly integrated into everyday life, their ability to provide safe and reliable assistance in realistic daily activities remains insufficiently explored. Existing safety benchmarks primarily focus on isolated harmful requests or adversarial scenarios, overlooking the safety risks that may arise as ordinary tasks unfold. In practice, users pursue legitimate goals through multi-turn, multimodal interactions, where seemingly benign requests may become safety-critical when considered alongside earlier actions and visual observations. This calls for Dynamic Safety Awareness, the ability to track evolving task conditions, recognize implicit contextual risks, and provide safe and helpful assistance. Grounded in authentic user dialogue topics and documented safety knowledge, DailySafe captures realistic scenarios where safety-critical evidence is progressively revealed across conversational turns and visual observations. A fixed paired-teacher filter retains samples answered correctly from an evidence-centered positive crop but incorrectly from a spatially displaced negative crop. At the token level, both EMA teachers score the same student-generated response, and their absolute log-probability differences measure view sensitivity. Additional supervision targets tokens admitted by sensitivity or teacher-distribution disagreement, with shared frozen-prior, template-bigram, and student--teacher divergence gates. Inverse-frequency redistribution preserves the total additional weight within each micro-batch, while the positive teacher remains the sole distillation target. Inference requires only the full-image student. Across eight benchmarks, our 4B and 9B models achieve macro-averages of 81.44 and 84.92, outperforming Vision-OPD reproductions by 0.97 and 0.52 points, respectively. Code on GitHub: https://github.com/Kaelyn01/VisAlloc</p></details> |  |
| **[Ultimate3D: Teaching Multimodal LLMs 3D Camera-Object Relations by Large-Scale Visual Instruction Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24795v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with accurately capturing camera-object relations, especially for object orientation, camera viewpoint, and camera shots. Additionally, large language models (LLMs) are used to generate text prompts for guiding visual instruction tuning and controlling image generation. We create Ultimate3D, a large-scale 3D-grounded 2D visual instruction dataset. Ultimate3D consists of 240K VQAs with precise camera-object annotations, and corresponding benchmark. MLLMs fine-tuned on our proposed dataset outperform commercial models by a large margin, achieving an average accuracy improvement of 29.7% on camera-object relation recognition tasks. Our code, dataset, and benchmark will contribute to broad MLLM applications.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to Machine Vision and Applications 2027</p></details> |
| **[A$^3$Bench: Compositional Reasoning over Aggressive Interactions in Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24762v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on general video understanding benchmarks, yet their ability to reason about participant roles in aggressive interactions remains largely unexplored. | **[AERR-Nav: Adaptive Exploration-Recovery-Reminiscing Strategy for Zero-Shot Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17712v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Zero-Shot Object Navigation (ZSON) in unknown multi-floor environments presents a significant challenge. | **[CRAM: Centroid-Routing and Adaptive MoE for Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02502v4)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) unify heterogeneous vision-language tasks under a shared generative framework via instruction tuning, yet real-world deployment demands continuous capability expansion, making Multimodal Continual Instruction Tuning (MCIT) essential. Extensive experiments across diverse benchmarks demonstrate its superiority over existing methods. | **[Visual Token Coding for Video Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28008v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this paper, we propose a new token compression paradigm for video Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), termed Visual Token Coding ($VTC$). | **[WM^MLLM: Turning MLLMs into World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24193v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Video world models keep the world in the context of a generator, so a revisit is synthesized again and earlier edits can drift or disappear. WM^MLLM keeps the world state outside every generator, as a panorama with a revision history, and lets the MLLMs write it: they turn each request into operations on this state, select where a local edit is written, and check every candidate before it becomes the next state. By construction, a revisit at the same revision reproduces the earlier view exactly, a local edit leaves every panorama pixel outside its region unchanged, and each request starts from the result of the previous one. It has the best value in all 15 CompBench columns against 15 baselines. On 112 local edits judged blind, selecting the region to write after generation raises completion from 34.8% to 83.0%. On 258 WBench and MBench-A items, all 46 revisits recover their earlier views, and all 97 local edits that pass the check leave panorama pixels outside their region unchanged.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 14 figures, 8 tables; project page: https://ztmotalee.github.io/wmmllm/</p></details> |
| **[DLWM: Diverse Latent World Models for Efficient Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.15160v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have improved considerably in recent years. In real-world multimodal settings, visual observations are often subject to occlusion, blur, viewpoint variation, or semantic ambiguity, giving rise to multiple plausible interpretations. A uniform reasoning strategy not only limits the model's ability to explore multiple hypotheses but also incurs high memory usage and rollout cost. We present DLWM (Diverse Latent World Models), a multimodal reasoning framework that combines latent-space reasoning with reinforcement learning. First, we construct a set of diverse latent world hypotheses in continuous latent space, each representing a candidate interpretation of the visual input, and unfold latent reasoning independently on each hypothesis. An orthogonality-based diversity regularizer discourages hypothesis collapse. Second, we formulate the latent reasoning process as a resource-constrained sequential decision problem and introduce a resource-aware reinforcement learning policy that adaptively allocates computation across hypotheses, dynamically deciding whether to expand a reasoning path, prune a hypothesis, merge reasoning paths, or produce the final answer, thereby substantially reducing memory footprint and improving rollout efficiency. Experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that DLWM outperforms existing methods by 2-5 points in accuracy while reducing memory usage by 24%.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 2 figures. Project page: https://dawae111.github.io/DLWM/</p></details> |
| **[Benchmarking Reasoning Reliability,Prior_Evidence Contrast,and Spectral Discrimination in Remote-Sensing Geological Image Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24068v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable lithology interpretation requires accurate geological recognition and reasoning grounded in visual evidence. | **[CausalSpatial: A Benchmark for Object-Centric Causal Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13304v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans can look at a static scene and instantly predict what happens next -- will moving this object cause a collision? We introduce CausalSpatial, a diagnostic benchmark evaluating whether models can anticipate consequences of object motions across four tasks: Collision, Compatibility, Occlusion, and Trajectory. Our analysis uncovers a fundamental deficiency: models over-rely on textual chain-of-thought reasoning that drifts from visual evidence, producing fluent but spatially ungrounded hallucinations. To address this, we propose the Causal Object World model (COW), a framework that externalizes the simulation process by generating videos of hypothetical dynamics. With explicit visual cues of causality, COW enables models to ground their reasoning in physical reality rather than linguistic priors. We make the dataset and code publicly available here: https://github.com/CausalSpatial/CausalSpatial</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code is available: https://github.com/CausalSpatial/CausalSpatial</p></details> |
| **[Shared Context, Separate Engines: Stateful Streaming Inference for Multimodal LLMs on Edge SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35309v5)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Streaming multimodal large language models (MLLMs) must take in new video frames while answering questions about what they have already seen. On an edge SoC, if one engine does both, ingestion and answering compete for its time while the other engines sit idle. To take over a query, another engine must first import or rebuild the accumulated history, because each runtime keeps it as KV state in its own format. \name lets one engine answer from history that another engine built. Each query reads the history as it stood when the query was asked, so one engine can keep ingesting while another answers. The CPU and GPU can also decode answers to different queries simultaneously. Phase-level state continuation lets an engine prefill a query, pass its KV state to another engine for decoding, and return to ingestion. State-Driven Execution Planning (SDEP) chooses where each query is prefilled and decoded, based on the state each engine holds and the work queued on it. Across M4 Max, RK3588, and QCS6490, \name outperforms single-engine execution. On a live M4 Max stream, SDEP cuts p95 time to first token by $76\%$, and every query receives its first token within one second. Under backlog, it raises request throughput by up to $35\%$. For two concurrent video streams on RK3588, SDEP delivers $19.6\%$ higher request throughput than single-engine execution and $27.2\%$ more than running both engines in parallel without shared state. \name delivers up to $2.48\times$ the throughput of the same engines run serially. Every answer matches serial execution token for token.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multimodal Information Fusion for Chart Understanding: A Survey of MLLMs -- Evolution, Limitations, and Cognitive Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10138v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chart understanding is a quintessential information fusion task, requiring the seamless integration of graphical and textual data to extract meaning. The advent of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has revolutionized this domain, yet the landscape of MLLM-based chart analysis remains fragmented and lacks systematic organization. This survey provides a comprehensive roadmap of this nascent frontier by structuring the domain's core components. We begin by analyzing the fundamental challenges of fusing visual and linguistic information in charts. Subsequently, we present a comprehensive evolution of methodologies, tracing the progression from classic deep learning techniques to state-of-the-art MLLM paradigms that leverage sophisticated fusion strategies. By critically examining the limitations of current models, particularly their perceptual and reasoning deficits, we identify promising future directions, including advanced alignment techniques and reinforcement learning for cognitive enhancement. This survey aims to equip researchers and practitioners with a structured understanding of how MLLMs are transforming chart information fusion and to catalyze progress toward more robust and reliable systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scenes as an Attack Surface: Uncovering Scene-Grounded Safety Failures in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14094v2)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing jailbreak attacks have exposed serious safety weaknesses in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). ## Open Vocabulary Segmentation - 100 in Total
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Segment Anything In-Context via Visual Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29896v1)** | 2026-11-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present the Segment Anything Model via Visual Demonstrations (SAYD), a foundation model for in-context segmentation. Given visual demos comprising prompt images and their segmentation masks, SAYD segments coherent regions (e.g., objects or their parts) in a query image by establishing visual correspondences between the prompts and the query. SAYD generalizes to previously-unseen segmentation tasks, including those requiring tacit visual knowledge or involving concepts that are difficult to articulate unambiguously in language. We train SAYD on 12 public datasets originally curated for panoptic segmentation using our proposed techniques, including a lightweight mask decoder for prompt-agnostic mask generation and a learning mechanism for recognizing novel query classes absent in the prompts. These innovations enhance both computational efficiency and out-of-domain generalization. Consequently, SAYD significantly outperforms existing methods of in-context segmentation and open-vocabulary segmentation across 24 out-of-domain benchmarks, spanning natural, biological, ecological, cosmological, medical, pathological, and satellite imagery.</p></details> |  |
| **[PartPO: Part-Level Policy Optimization for Shape-to-Program Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.29213v1)** | 2026-11-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present PartPO, a reinforcement learning method for generating structured shape programs from 3D point clouds. Existing shape-to-program models are trained with supervised learning on procedurally generated data and generalize poorly to more diverse shapes, while reinforcement learning from execution feedback assigns a single scalar reward to a long program that builds many parts, making credit assignment difficult. PartPO instead builds on a core property of the task: shape programs are self-describing, with each semantically named part produced by its own code block. It segments each generated program by its parts and applies part-level rewards only to the code of the corresponding part, a category reward only to the object name, and a shape-level reward to the whole program. The required part supervision is obtained without manual annotation, by grounding part names on target meshes with an open-vocabulary segmentation model prompt-tuned on the model's own outputs. Applied to MeshCoder, PartPO improves reconstruction accuracy and execution success on ShapeNetCore and ABO over the base model, rejection-sampling fine-tuning and trajectory-level RL, and generalizes to the unseen HSSD dataset. Code will be released at https://github.com/HannuoZ/PartPO.</p></details> |  |
| **[What the Background Hides: Thresholds, Vocabularies and Collisions in Training-Free Open-Vocabulary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.28966v1)** | 2026-11-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training-free open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) built on CLIP has advanced rapidly, with methods typically ranked by mean intersection-over-union (mIoU) across standard benchmarks. On datasets with a background class, however, implementations rely on per-dataset thresholds and background word lists that are rarely documented or unified (11 of 13 surveyed methods configure per-dataset background decisions). We audit configurations and papers of 13 training-free OVSS methods, re-run seven from official codebases, and compare official settings against a strict unified protocol under pre-registered contracts. The protocol gap depends strongly on the benchmark---ranging from 22.8 mIoU points on PASCAL VOC (VOC21) to 0.1 points (indistinguishable from zero) on ADE20K---and alters pairwise method rankings. Despite large shifts in absolute mIoU, most of the reported improvement of later CLIP-only methods over SCLIP remains under the unified protocol (roughly 79% of the published gain). Decomposing official background handling shows that it combines numeric settings and class-name files whose dominant component reverses across benchmarks, while no tested tuning-free rule transfers reliably across all datasets. Moreover, exact collisions between background words and target names---present in six of seven official COCO-Object configurations through "bed"---make affected classes unpredictable under official post-processing, while the main per-class drops under language-model-generated vocabularies on VOC21 are largely attributable to a few generated terms. We offer reporting recommendations and release our evaluation code, frozen configurations, contracts, and a collision-check tool.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 11 figures, 13 tables. Submitted to Computer Vision and Image Understanding. Code: https://github.com/WendongYao/ovss-background-audit ; data: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24338613</p></details> |
| **[Bridging vision-language reasoning and learned control for legged navigation in the wild](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25215v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous navigation in diverse real-world environments remains one of the greatest challenges in robotics. An open-vocabulary segmentation module converts these labels into a dense, real-time risk map, which conditions two pre-trained RL policies responsible for agile locomotion and local navigation, effectively coupling high-level VLM reasoning with low-level physical control. Real-world experiments showed the system's ability to autonomously navigate a wide range of complex real-world environments. The robot completes kilometer-long hikes with minimal intervention, interprets signs to avoid danger, and evaluates its environment to identify safe areas on which to step. This work represents a step toward the reliable deployment of systems in unstructured environments. However, it also underscores the challenges that remain in VLM reliability and spatial reasoning, indicating that foundational models are a crucial component but not yet a complete solution for navigation in the wild.</p></details> | <details><summary>42 pa...</summary><p>42 pages, 6 figures, 15 supplementary figures. | **[Pixel2Perspective: A Benchmark for Multi-Modal and Multi-View Generalization in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24327v1)** | 2026-11-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI systems, such as drones and autonomous vehicles, improve navigation by enhancing perception in environments containing unseen objects. For effective deployment, open-vocabulary semantic segmentation (OVSS) models, leveraging models like CLIP to assign pixel-level labels from text, must be robust to outdoor conditions, adverse weather, and varying viewpoints. Despite their potential, these models encounter significant challenges in cross-domain generalization, hindering their practical use in embodied AI applications. In this work, we present Pixel2Perspective, a benchmark for multi-angle object localization and open-vocabulary segmentation across both aerial and ground perspectives. This benchmark supports thorough evaluation across different viewpoints and sensor modalities. We assess foundational models like CLIP and advanced OVSS models using a curated dataset that spans varied outdoor conditions, sim-to-real scenarios, and multiple sensors, including infrared, to support the development of robust and generalizable perception systems. We also present a novel framework, Multi-Source RGB-IR Uncertainty Quantification (MSRIQ), which jointly models aleatoric and epistemic uncertainties across RGB and infrared imagery. This approach supports robust evaluation of open-vocabulary segmentation models across diverse environmental conditions. Our work sets a robustness-driven benchmark, providing key insights and laying the foundation for future research in open-world embodied AI perception. | **[Text-Guided Modality Correction for Open-Vocabulary Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22126v1)** | 2026-11-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-vocabulary object detection (OVD) is essential for detecting objects beyond the categories seen during training. Second, a cross-modal alignment module strengthens the correspondence between region representations and category semantics through interactions between visual and textual features. Finally, a progressive text correction decoder combines a top-down pathway with lateral connections to progressively fuse text-guided deep semantic features with shallow visual features at the corresponding scales, preserving both semantic information and spatial detail for objects of different sizes. Experiments on two public datasets, COCO-OVD and LVIS-OVD, demonstrate that TMCNet achieves strong performance in open-vocabulary object detection using simple text queries as guidance.
